Explanation. We need $x - 1 > 0$; since $\log_2$ is increasing, $\log_2(x-1) < 3 \iff x - 1 < 8$. Hence $1 < x < 9$.
am1-f-500 · level facile · notes
For which base $a$ is the exponential function $f(x) = a^x$ decreasing on $\R$?
  1. $a = \frac32$
  2. $a = \frac23$
  3. $a = e$
  4. $a = 1$
Explanation. $a^x$ is increasing for $a > 1$, decreasing for $0 < a < 1$ and constant for $a = 1$.
am1-f-501 · level facile · notes
What are the real solutions of the equation $e^{2x} - 3e^{x} + 2 = 0$ ($\log$ is the natural logarithm)?
  1. $x = 1$ and $x = 2$
  2. $x = 0$ and $x = 2$
  3. $x = 0$ and $x = \log 2$
  4. only $x = \log 2$
Explanation. With $t = e^x > 0$ we get $t^2 - 3t + 2 = 0$, i.e. $t = 1$ or $t = 2$; hence $x = 0$ or $x = \log 2$. $1$ and $2$ are the values of $t$, not of $x$.
am1-f-502 · level facile · notes
Given $\log 2 \approx 0.693$ and $\log 10 \approx 2.303$ (natural $\log$), what is $\log_{2} 10$ approximately?
  1. $\approx 0.30$
  2. $\approx 1.60$
  3. $\approx 5$
  4. $\approx 3.32$
Explanation. Change of base: $\log_2 10 = \frac{\log 10}{\log 2} \approx \frac{2.303}{0.693} \approx 3.32$. The value $0.30$ is $\log_{10} 2$, the reciprocal.
am1-f-503 · level facile · notes
For which $x$ is $\log_{1/3} x > 0$?
  1. $x > 1$
  2. $0 < x < 1$
  3. $x > \frac13$
  4. $0 < x < \frac13$
Explanation. With base $\frac13 < 1$ the logarithm is decreasing and equals $0$ at $x = 1$: it is positive for $0 < x < 1$.

6. Trigonometric functions (12)

am1-f-045 · level facile · notes
Double-angle formula: $\cos(2x)$ equals:
  1. $2 \sin x \cos x$
  2. $\cos^2 x - \sin^2 x$
  3. $2\cos^2 x + 1$
  4. $\cos^2 x + \sin^2 x$
Explanation. $\cos(2x) = \cos^2 x - \sin^2 x = 2\cos^2 x - 1$. $2\sin x\cos x$ is $\sin(2x)$.
am1-f-046 · level facile · notes
What is $\cos\frac{2\pi}{3}$?
  1. $-\frac12$
  2. $\frac12$
  3. $-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}$
  4. $\frac{\sqrt3}{2}$
Explanation. $\frac{2\pi}{3} = \pi - \frac\pi3$, hence $\cos\frac{2\pi}{3} = -\cos\frac\pi3 = -\frac12$.
am1-f-221 · level facile · notes
What is $\tan\frac{5\pi}{4}$?
  1. $-1$
  2. $\frac{\sqrt2}{2}$
  3. $\sqrt3$
  4. $1$
Explanation. $\tan$ has period $\pi$: $\tan\frac{5\pi}{4} = \tan\frac{\pi}{4} = 1$ (sine and cosine are both $-\frac{\sqrt2}{2}$).
am1-f-222 · level facile · notes
Addition formula: $\sin(\alpha + \beta)$ equals:
  1. $\sin\alpha\cos\beta + \cos\alpha\sin\beta$
  2. $\sin\alpha + \sin\beta$
  3. $\sin\alpha\sin\beta + \cos\alpha\cos\beta$
  4. $\sin\alpha\cos\beta - \cos\alpha\sin\beta$
Explanation. This is the sine addition formula. $\cos\alpha\cos\beta + \sin\alpha\sin\beta$ is $\cos(\alpha - \beta)$, and the minus sign gives $\sin(\alpha - \beta)$.
am1-f-223 · level facile · notes
What are the solutions of the equation $\sin x = \frac12$ in the interval $[0, 2\pi)$?
  1. $x = \frac\pi6$
  2. $x = \frac\pi6$ and $x = \frac{5\pi}{6}$
  3. $x = \frac\pi3$ and $x = \frac{2\pi}{3}$
  4. $x = \frac\pi6$ and $x = \frac{7\pi}{6}$
Explanation. $\sin\frac\pi6 = \frac12$ and, since $\sin(\pi - x) = \sin x$, also $\sin\frac{5\pi}{6} = \frac12$. At $\frac{7\pi}6$ the sine equals $-\frac12$.
am1-f-224 · level facile · notes
If $\cos x = \frac35$ and $x \in \left(-\frac\pi2, 0\right)$, what is $\sin x$?
  1. $\frac45$
  2. $\frac25$
  3. $-\frac45$
  4. $-\frac35$
Explanation. $\sin^2 x = 1 - \frac{9}{25} = \frac{16}{25}$, and in the fourth quadrant the sine is negative: $\sin x = -\frac45$.
am1-f-225 · level facile · notes
A harmonic oscillation is described by $y(t) = 3 \sin(4t + 1)$. What are its amplitude and period?
  1. amplitude $3$, period $4$
  2. amplitude $3$, period $\frac\pi2$
  3. amplitude $4$, period $\frac{2\pi}{3}$
  4. amplitude $3$, period $8\pi$
Explanation. In $A\sin(\omega t + \varphi)$ the amplitude is $|A| = 3$ and the period is $\frac{2\pi}{\omega} = \frac{2\pi}{4} = \frac\pi2$; the phase $1$ does not change the period.
am1-f-226 · level facile · notes
Which identity holds for every $x$ where both sides are defined?
  1. $1 + \tan^2 x = \frac{1}{\sin^2 x}$
  2. $\sin(2x) = 2\sin x$
  3. $\cos(x + \pi) = \cos x$
  4. $1 + \tan^2 x = \frac{1}{\cos^2 x}$
Explanation. Dividing $\sin^2 x + \cos^2 x = 1$ by $\cos^2 x$ gives $\tan^2 x + 1 = \frac{1}{\cos^2 x}$. Instead $\sin 2x = 2\sin x\cos x$ and $\cos(x + \pi) = -\cos x$.
am1-f-504 · level facile · notes
On the unit circle (centre the origin, radius $1$), the point associated with the angle $x$ (in radians, measured counterclockwise from the positive $x$-axis) has coordinates:
  1. $(\cos x, \sin x)$
  2. $(\sin x, \cos x)$
  3. $(\cos x, \tan x)$
  4. $(x, \sin x)$
Explanation. By definition the cosine is the abscissa and the sine the ordinate of the point of the unit circle determined by the angle $x$.
am1-f-505 · level facile · notes
What is the domain of the function $\tan x = \frac{\sin x}{\cos x}$?
  1. $\R \setminus \{k\pi : k \in \Z\}$
  2. $\R \setminus \left\{\frac\pi2 + 2k\pi : k \in \Z\right\}$
  3. $\R$
  4. $\R \setminus \left\{\frac\pi2 + k\pi : k \in \Z\right\}$
Explanation. The zeros of the cosine, i.e. $\frac\pi2 + k\pi$, must be excluded. The zeros of the sine $k\pi$ are excluded for the cotangent.
am1-f-506 · level facile · notes
A harmonic oscillation $y(t) = A \sin(\omega t + \varphi)$, with $A > 0$ and $\omega > 0$, has period $T = \frac{2\pi}{\omega}$ and frequency $\frac1T$. If $T = 0.5$ seconds, what are the frequency and $\omega$?
  1. frequency $0.5$, $\omega = \pi$
  2. frequency $2$, $\omega = 2$
  3. frequency $2$, $\omega = 4\pi$
  4. frequency $2$, $\omega = \pi$
Explanation. The frequency is $\frac{1}{0.5} = 2$ oscillations per second and $\omega = \frac{2\pi}{T} = 4\pi$.
am1-f-507 · level facile · notes
Using the double-angle formula $\sin(2x) = 2\sin x\cos x$, what is $\sin\frac{\pi}{12}\,\cos\frac{\pi}{12}$?
  1. $\frac12$
  2. $\frac14$
  3. $\frac{\sqrt3}{4}$
  4. $\frac{\sqrt3}{2}$
Explanation. $\sin\frac{\pi}{12}\cos\frac{\pi}{12} = \frac12 \sin\frac{\pi}{6} = \frac12 \cdot \frac12 = \frac14$.

7. Integer part and fractional part functions (12)

am1-f-047 · level facile · notes
The fractional part function $(x) = x - [x]$, where $[x]$ is the integer part (the largest integer $\le x$), takes values in:
  1. $[0, 1)$
  2. $(0, 1]$
  3. $[0, 1]$
  4. $(-1, 1)$
Explanation. From $[x] \le x < [x] + 1$ we get $0 \le x - [x] < 1$; it is $0$ exactly at the integers.
am1-f-048 · level facile · notes
As a piecewise-defined function, $|x - 2|$ equals:
  1. $x - 2$ for $x < 2$ and $2 - x$ for $x \ge 2$
  2. $x + 2$ for $x < -2$ and $x - 2$ for $x \ge -2$
  3. $2 - x$ for $x < 2$ and $x - 2$ for $x \ge 2$
  4. $x - 2$ for every $x$
Explanation. For $x \ge 2$ the argument is $\ge 0$ and stays unchanged; for $x < 2$ it is negative and changes sign: $2 - x$.
am1-f-227 · level facile · notes
With $[x]$ the integer part ($[x]$ is the integer $n$ with $n \le x < n+1$), what is $[-2.5] + [2.5]$?
  1. $0$
  2. $1$
  3. $-1$
  4. $-2$
Explanation. $[2.5] = 2$ and $[-2.5] = -3$ (not $-2$: we need $n \le -2.5$). The sum is $-1$.
am1-f-228 · level facile · notes
With $[x]$ the integer part ($n \le x < n+1$), what is the solution set of the equation $[x] = 3$?
  1. $[3, 4)$
  2. $\{3\}$
  3. $(3, 4]$
  4. $[2, 3)$
Explanation. $[x] = 3 \iff 3 \le x < 4$: the integer part is piecewise constant, with jumps at the integers.
am1-f-229 · level facile · notes
Is the fractional part function $(x) = x - [x]$, with $[x]$ the integer part, periodic? With which minimal period?
  1. it is not periodic
  2. yes, period $2$
  3. yes, period $\frac12$
  4. yes, period $1$
Explanation. $[x + 1] = [x] + 1$, so $(x + 1) = (x)$; no $T \in (0, 1)$ works since $(0) = 0$ and $(T) = T \neq 0$.
am1-f-230 · level facile · notes
What is the solution set of the inequality $|2x - 3| \ge 5$?
  1. $[-1, 4]$
  2. $(-\infty, -1] \cup [4, \ip)$
  3. $[4, \ip)$
  4. $(-\infty, -4] \cup [1, \ip)$
Explanation. $|2x - 3| \ge 5 \iff 2x - 3 \le -5$ or $2x - 3 \ge 5$, i.e. $x \le -1$ or $x \ge 4$.
am1-f-231 · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = x^2$ for $x \le 1$ and $f(x) = 3 - x$ for $x > 1$. What is $f(-2) + f(4)$?
  1. $5$
  2. $-3$
  3. $3$
  4. $15$
Explanation. $-2 \le 1$, so $f(-2) = 4$; $4 > 1$, so $f(4) = 3 - 4 = -1$. The sum is $3$.
am1-f-232 · level facile · notes
For which $x \in \R$ does the equality $|x - 1| = 1 - x$ hold?
  1. $x \le 1$
  2. $x \ge 1$
  3. only $x = 1$
  4. for every $x \in \R$
Explanation. $|a| = -a$ exactly when $a \le 0$: here $a = x - 1 \le 0$, i.e. $x \le 1$.
am1-f-508 · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = 2x + 1$ for $x < 0$ and $f(x) = 1 - x^2$ for $x \ge 0$. What is the solution set of $f(x) = 0$?
  1. $\left\{-\frac12, 1\right\}$
  2. $\left\{-\frac12\right\}$
  3. $\{-1, 1\}$
  4. $\left\{-\frac12, -1, 1\right\}$
Explanation. For $x < 0$: $2x + 1 = 0$ gives $x = -\frac12$, admissible. For $x \ge 0$: $1 - x^2 = 0$ gives $x = 1$ ($x = -1$ is discarded, since it is not $\ge 0$).
am1-f-509 · level facile · notes
What is the piecewise expression of the function $f(x) = x + |x|$?
  1. $2x$ for $x < 0$ and $0$ for $x \ge 0$
  2. $0$ for every $x$
  3. $2x$ for every $x$
  4. $0$ for $x < 0$ and $2x$ for $x \ge 0$
Explanation. For $x < 0$ we have $|x| = -x$ and $f(x) = 0$; for $x \ge 0$ we have $|x| = x$ and $f(x) = 2x$.
am1-f-510 · level facile · notes
With $[x]$ the integer part (the largest integer $\le x$), what is the solution set of the equation $[x]^2 = 4$?
  1. $\{-2, 2\}$
  2. $[2, 3)$
  3. $[-2, -1) \cup [2, 3)$
  4. $(-3, -2] \cup [2, 3)$
Explanation. $[x]^2 = 4$ means $[x] = 2$ or $[x] = -2$, i.e. $2 \le x < 3$ or $-2 \le x < -1$.
am1-f-511 · level facile · notes
For which $x \in \R$ does $|x + 1| + |x - 1| = 2$ hold?
  1. $\{-1, 1\}$
  2. $[-1, 1]$
  3. $\{0\}$
  4. $\R$
Explanation. $|x + 1| + |x - 1|$ is the sum of the distances of $x$ from $-1$ and from $1$: it equals $2$ for $x \in [-1, 1]$ and is larger than $2$ outside this interval.

8. Hyperbolic functions (12)

am1-f-049 · level facile · notes
With $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$ and $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$, which identity holds for every $x \in \R$?
  1. $\cosH^2 x + \sinH^2 x = 1$
  2. $\cosH^2 x - \sinH^2 x = -1$
  3. $\cosH^2 x - \sinH^2 x = \cosH (2x)$
  4. $\cosH^2 x - \sinH^2 x = 1$
Explanation. With $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}2$ and $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}2$: $\cosH^2 x - \sinH^2 x = \frac{4}{4} = 1$ (hyperbola $X^2 - Y^2 = 1$).
am1-f-050 · level facile · notes
Hyperbolic sine $\sinH x$ and hyperbolic cosine $\cosH x$: which statement is correct?
  1. $\sinH$ is odd and $\cosH$ is even
  2. both are even
  3. $\sinH$ is even and $\cosH$ is odd
  4. both are odd
Explanation. Replacing $x$ with $-x$ swaps $e^x$ and $e^{-x}$: $\sinH(-x) = -\sinH x$ and $\cosH(-x) = \cosH x$.
am1-f-233 · level facile · notes
What is $\cosH(\log 2)$, with $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$ and $\log$ the natural logarithm?
  1. $\frac32$
  2. $\frac54$
  3. $\frac34$
  4. $1$
Explanation. $e^{\log 2} = 2$ and $e^{-\log 2} = \frac12$: $\cosH(\log 2) = \frac{2 + \frac12}{2} = \frac54$.
am1-f-234 · level facile · notes
What is the image of the hyperbolic cosine $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$, $x \in \R$?
  1. $\R$
  2. $[-1, 1]$
  3. $(0, \ip)$
  4. $[1, \ip)$
Explanation. By the arithmetic-geometric mean inequality $\frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2} \ge \sqrt{e^x e^{-x}} = 1$, with equality at $x = 0$; and $\cosH x \rr \ip$ as $x \rr \pm\infty$.
am1-f-235 · level facile · notes
With $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$ and $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$, what is $\cosH x + \sinH x$?
  1. $e^x$
  2. $e^{-x}$
  3. $1$
  4. $2\,e^x$
Explanation. Adding the two definitions the $e^{-x}$ terms cancel: $\frac{2e^x}{2} = e^x$. Instead $\cosH x - \sinH x = e^{-x}$.
am1-f-236 · level facile · notes
Duplication formula for the hyperbolic sine $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$: $\sinH(2x)$ equals:
  1. $2\sinH x$
  2. $\cosH^2 x - \sinH^2 x$
  3. $2\sinH x\,\cosH x$
  4. $\sinH^2 x + \cosH^2 x$
Explanation. $2\sinH x\cosH x = 2\,\frac{(e^x - e^{-x})(e^x + e^{-x})}{4} = \frac{e^{2x} - e^{-2x}}{2} = \sinH(2x)$. $\cosH^2 x + \sinH^2 x$ is $\cosH(2x)$.
am1-f-237 · level facile · notes
The hyperbolic tangent $\tanH x = \frac{\sinH x}{\cosH x}$, $x \in \R$, has image:
  1. $\R$
  2. $(-1, 1)$
  3. $[-1, 1]$
  4. $(0, 1)$
Explanation. $\tanH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{e^x + e^{-x}}$ is increasing, odd, and tends to $\pm 1$ as $x \rr \pm\infty$ without attaining these values.
am1-f-238 · level facile · notes
For which $x \in \R$ is $\sinH x = 0$, with $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$?
  1. for $x = k\pi$, $k \in \Z$
  2. for no $x$
  3. for $x = 0$ and $x = \log 2$
  4. only for $x = 0$
Explanation. $e^x - e^{-x} = 0 \iff e^{2x} = 1 \iff x = 0$. Unlike the sine, the hyperbolic sine is strictly increasing and has a single zero.
am1-f-512 · level facile · notes
The hyperbolic sine $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$, $x \in \R$, is:
  1. increasing on $\R$, with image $(-1, 1)$
  2. decreasing on $(-\infty, 0]$ and increasing on $[0, \ip)$
  3. periodic with period $2\pi$
  4. increasing on $\R$, with image $\R$
Explanation. $e^x$ is increasing and $e^{-x}$ decreasing, so their difference is increasing; moreover $\sinH x \rr \pm\infty$ as $x \rr \pm\infty$. The image $(-1, 1)$ is that of the hyperbolic tangent.
am1-f-513 · level facile · notes
With $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$ and $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$, what is $\cosH(2x)$ equal to?
  1. $\cosH^2 x - \sinH^2 x$
  2. $2\cosH x$
  3. $\cosH^2 x + \sinH^2 x$
  4. $1 - 2\sinH^2 x$
Explanation. $\cosH^2 x + \sinH^2 x = \frac{(e^{2x} + 2 + e^{-2x}) + (e^{2x} - 2 + e^{-2x})}{4} = \frac{e^{2x} + e^{-2x}}{2}$. The difference equals $1$; $1 - 2\sinH^2 x$ wrongly copies the cosine formula.
am1-f-514 · level facile · notes
What is $\sinH(\log 3)$, with $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$ and $\log$ the natural logarithm?
  1. $\frac53$
  2. $\frac43$
  3. $1$
  4. $\frac83$
Explanation. $e^{\log 3} = 3$ and $e^{-\log 3} = \frac13$, so $\sinH(\log 3) = \frac{3 - \frac13}{2} = \frac43$. $\frac53$ is $\cosH(\log 3)$, $\frac83$ forgets the factor $\frac12$.
am1-f-515 · level facile · notes
With $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$ and $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$, what is the addition formula for $\sinH(x + y)$?
  1. $\sinH x \cosH y + \cosH x \sinH y$
  2. $\sinH x \cosH y - \cosH x \sinH y$
  3. $\cosH x \cosH y + \sinH x \sinH y$
  4. $\sinH x + \sinH y$
Explanation. Expanding with exponentials gives the same form as the sine formula: $\sinH(x + y) = \sinH x \cosH y + \cosH x \sinH y$. The third expression is $\cosH(x + y)$.

9. Operations on graphs (12)

am1-f-051 · level facile · notes
The graph of $y = f(x + 3)$ is obtained from that of $y = f(x)$ by:
  1. a translation to the right by $3$
  2. a translation to the left by $3$
  3. an upward translation by $3$
  4. a downward translation by $3$
Explanation. The value taken by $f$ at $x_0$ is now taken at $x_0 - 3$: the graph moves to the left. Moving it to the right is the typical mistake.
am1-f-052 · level facile · notes
The graph of $y = f(|x|)$ is obtained from that of $y = f(x)$ by:
  1. reflecting across the $x$-axis the parts where $f(x) < 0$
  2. reflecting the whole graph across the $y$-axis
  3. keeping the part with $x \ge 0$ and reflecting it across the $y$-axis
  4. keeping the part with $x \le 0$ and reflecting it across the $x$-axis
Explanation. $f(|x|)$ is even and coincides with $f$ for $x \ge 0$. Reflecting the negative parts across the $x$-axis gives $|f(x)|$ instead.
am1-f-239 · level facile · notes
The graph of $y = -f(x)$ is obtained from that of $y = f(x)$ by:
  1. a reflection in the $y$-axis
  2. a reflection in the origin
  3. a reflection in the $x$-axis
  4. a downward translation
Explanation. The point $(x, f(x))$ becomes $(x, -f(x))$: the ordinate changes sign, i.e. the graph is reflected in the $x$-axis.
am1-f-240 · level facile · notes
The graph of $y = f(2x)$ is obtained from that of $y = f(x)$ by:
  1. a horizontal compression by a factor $2$ (abscissas are halved)
  2. a horizontal stretch by a factor $2$ (abscissas are doubled)
  3. a vertical stretch by a factor $2$
  4. a translation to the left by $2$
Explanation. The value taken by $f$ at $x_0$ is now taken at $\frac{x_0}{2}$: the graph is compressed horizontally toward the $y$-axis.
am1-f-241 · level facile · notes
The graph of $y = |f(x)|$ is obtained from that of $y = f(x)$:
  1. by keeping the part with $x \ge 0$ and reflecting it in the $y$-axis
  2. by reflecting the whole graph in the $x$-axis
  3. by deleting the parts with $f(x) < 0$
  4. by reflecting in the $x$-axis the parts with $f(x) < 0$
Explanation. Where $f(x) \ge 0$ the graph is unchanged, where $f(x) < 0$ the ordinate changes sign. The first option describes $f(|x|)$ instead.
am1-f-242 · level facile · notes
The parabola $y = x^2$ is translated $2$ to the right and $3$ down. What is the equation of the new curve?
  1. $y = (x + 2)^2 - 3$
  2. $y = (x - 2)^2 - 3$
  3. $y = (x - 2)^2 + 3$
  4. $y = x^2 - 2x - 3$
Explanation. Translating right by $2$ means replacing $x$ by $x - 2$; translating down by $3$ means subtracting $3$: vertex at $(2, -3)$.
am1-f-243 · level facile · notes
If the image of $f$ is $[-1, 2]$, what is the image of $g(x) = 3 f(x) + 1$?
  1. $[-3, 6]$
  2. $[0, 3]$
  3. $[-2, 7]$
  4. $[2, 5]$
Explanation. Vertical stretch by a factor $3$ and upward translation by $1$: the endpoints become $3(-1) + 1 = -2$ and $3 \cdot 2 + 1 = 7$.
am1-f-244 · level facile · notes
If $f$ has a unique zero at $x = 3$, where does $g(x) = f(x - 1)$ vanish?
  1. at $x = 4$
  2. at $x = 2$
  3. at $x = 3$
  4. at $x = -3$
Explanation. $g(x) = 0 \iff x - 1 = 3 \iff x = 4$: the graph of $f(x - 1)$ is that of $f$ translated $1$ to the right.
am1-f-516 · level facile · notes
The graph of $y = f(x) - 4$ is obtained from that of $y = f(x)$ by:
  1. a translation upwards by $4$
  2. a translation downwards by $4$
  3. a translation to the right by $4$
  4. a translation to the left by $4$
Explanation. Every ordinate decreases by $4$, the abscissas stay the same: it is a vertical translation downwards.
am1-f-517 · level facile · notes
The function $f$ vanishes exactly at $x = -6$ and $x = 9$. Where does $g(x) = f(3x)$ vanish?
  1. at $x = -18$ and $x = 27$
  2. at $x = -3$ and $x = 12$
  3. at $x = -2$ and $x = 3$
  4. at $x = -6$ and $x = 9$
Explanation. $g(x) = 0$ if and only if $3x = -6$ or $3x = 9$: the graph is compressed horizontally and the abscissas are divided by $3$.
am1-f-518 · level facile · notes
The image of $f$ is the interval $[-3, 2]$. What is the image of $g(x) = |f(x)|$?
  1. $[2, 3]$
  2. $[0, 2]$
  3. $[-3, 3]$
  4. $[0, 3]$
Explanation. The values of $f$ in $[-3, 0]$ become $[0, 3]$, those in $[0, 2]$ stay; the union is $[0, 3]$.
am1-f-519 · level facile · notes
The graph of $y = |\sin x|$ is obtained by reflecting in the $x$-axis the negative parts of $y = \sin x$. What is the (minimal) period of $|\sin x|$?
  1. $\pi$
  2. $2\pi$
  3. $\frac\pi2$
  4. $4\pi$
Explanation. $|\sin(x + \pi)| = |-\sin x| = |\sin x|$, and no smaller positive period works ($|\sin x|$ vanishes only at multiples of $\pi$).

10. Composite functions (12)

am1-f-053 · level facile · notes
With $f(x) = x^2$ and $g(x) = x + 1$, the composite function $g \circ f$ is:
  1. $(g \circ f)(x) = x^2 + 1$
  2. $(g \circ f)(x) = (x + 1)^2$
  3. $(g \circ f)(x) = x^2 + x$
  4. $(g \circ f)(x) = x^3 + x^2$
Explanation. $(g \circ f)(x) = g\big(f(x)\big)$: first apply $f$, then $g$. $(x+1)^2$ is $f \circ g$.
am1-f-054 · level facile · notes
With $f(x) = x - 3$ and $g(t) = \sqrt{t}$, for which $x$ is $g \circ f$ defined?
  1. for $x \ge 0$
  2. for $x \le 3$
  3. for every $x \in \R$
  4. for $x \ge 3$
Explanation. We need $f(x) \in [0, \ip)$, the domain of $g$: $x - 3 \ge 0$, i.e. $x \ge 3$.
am1-f-245 · level facile · notes
With $f(x) = 2x + 1$ and $g(x) = x^2$, what is $(f \circ g)(3) - (g \circ f)(3)$?
  1. $0$
  2. $30$
  3. $-19$
  4. $-30$
Explanation. $(f \circ g)(3) = f(9) = 19$ and $(g \circ f)(3) = g(7) = 49$: the difference is $-30$. Composition is not commutative.
am1-f-246 · level facile · notes
The function $h(x) = \sqrt{\log x}$ (natural $\log$) is written as a composition $h = g \circ f$ with:
  1. $f(x) = \log x$, $g(t) = \sqrt t$
  2. $f(x) = \sqrt x$, $g(t) = \log t$
  3. $f(x) = x$, $g(t) = \sqrt{t}\,\log t$
  4. $f(x) = \log x$, $g(t) = t^2$
Explanation. $(g \circ f)(x) = g(f(x))$: the logarithm is applied first, then the root. The reverse order gives $\log\sqrt x$.
am1-f-247 · level facile · notes
What is the domain of $h(x) = \sqrt{\log x}$ ($\log$ the natural logarithm)?
  1. $(0, \ip)$
  2. $[1, \ip)$
  3. $(1, \ip)$
  4. $[0, \ip)$
Explanation. We need $x > 0$ for the logarithm and $\log x \ge 0$ for the root, i.e. $x \ge 1$.
am1-f-248 · level teorema · notes
Let $f: A \rr B$ and $g: B \rr C$. Which statement about the composition $g \circ f: A \rr C$ is correct?
  1. If $g \circ f$ is injective, then $g$ is injective.
  2. If $f$ and $g$ are increasing, then $g \circ f$ is decreasing.
  3. If $f$ and $g$ are injective, then $g \circ f$ is injective.
  4. If $f$ and $g$ are decreasing, then $g \circ f$ is decreasing.
Explanation. From $g(f(x_1)) = g(f(x_2))$ we get $f(x_1) = f(x_2)$ and then $x_1 = x_2$. The composition of two decreasing functions is increasing; if $g \circ f$ is injective it is $f$ (not $g$) that must be injective.
am1-f-249 · level facile · notes
With $f(x) = \frac{1}{x}$ for $x \neq 0$, what is $(f \circ f)(x)$?
  1. $\frac{1}{x^2}$
  2. $1$
  3. $x$
  4. $\frac{2}{x}$
Explanation. $f(f(x)) = \frac{1}{1/x} = x$ for $x \neq 0$: $f$ is its own inverse. $\frac{1}{x^2}$ is the product $f \cdot f$, not the composition.
am1-f-250 · level facile · notes
With $f(x) = x + 2$ and $g(x) = 3x$, what is the expression of $(g \circ f)(x)$?
  1. $3x + 2$
  2. $3x^2 + 6x$
  3. $x + 6$
  4. $3x + 6$
Explanation. $(g \circ f)(x) = g(x + 2) = 3(x + 2) = 3x + 6$. $3x + 2$ is $f \circ g$.
am1-f-520 · level facile · notes
With $f(x) = x^2$ and $g(x) = x - 1$, for which $x$ is $(f \circ g)(x) = (g \circ f)(x)$?
  1. for every $x \in \R$
  2. only for $x = 1$
  3. only for $x = 0$
  4. for no $x$
Explanation. $(f \circ g)(x) = (x - 1)^2 = x^2 - 2x + 1$ and $(g \circ f)(x) = x^2 - 1$: they are equal if $-2x + 1 = -1$, i.e. $x = 1$. In general composition is not commutative.
am1-f-521 · level teorema · notes
Let $f$ and $g$ be real functions, with $g \circ f$ defined on an interval (“increasing” and “decreasing” in the strict sense). Which statement is correct?
  1. If $f$ and $g$ are decreasing, then $g \circ f$ is decreasing.
  2. If $f$ is increasing and $g$ is decreasing, then $g \circ f$ is increasing.
  3. If $f$ is increasing and $g$ is decreasing, then $g \circ f$ is decreasing.
  4. If $f$ and $g$ are increasing, then $g \circ f$ can be decreasing.
Explanation. If $x_1 < x_2$, then $f(x_1) < f(x_2)$ and, $g$ being decreasing, $g(f(x_1)) > g(f(x_2))$. Two decreasing functions instead give an increasing composite.
am1-f-522 · level facile · notes
What is the domain of $h(x) = \log(4 - x^2)$ (natural logarithm)?
  1. $[-2, 2]$
  2. $(-\infty, -2) \cup (2, \ip)$
  3. $(0, 2)$
  4. $(-2, 2)$
Explanation. $h = g \circ f$ with $f(x) = 4 - x^2$ and $g(t) = \log t$: we need $4 - x^2 > 0$, i.e. $-2 < x < 2$ (endpoints excluded).
am1-f-523 · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = 3x - 1$. Which function $g$ satisfies $(g \circ f)(x) = x$ for every $x \in \R$?
  1. $g(t) = \frac{t + 1}{3}$
  2. $g(t) = 3t + 1$
  3. $g(t) = \frac{t - 1}{3}$
  4. $g(t) = \frac{1}{3t - 1}$
Explanation. $g(f(x)) = \frac{(3x - 1) + 1}{3} = x$: $g$ is the inverse of $f$. The reciprocal $\frac{1}{3t - 1}$ is not the inverse.

11. Inverse functions (12)

am1-f-055 · level facile · notes
The arcsine is the function:
  1. $[-1, 1] \rr \left[-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right]$, $y \mt \arcsin y$
  2. $[-1, 1] \rr [0, \pi]$, $y \mt \arcsin y$
  3. $\R \rr \left(-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right)$, $y \mt \arcsin y$
  4. $\left[-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right] \rr [-1, 1]$, $y \mt \arcsin y$
Explanation. It is the inverse of the sine restricted to $\left[-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right]$: its domain is $[-1,1]$. $[0,\pi]$ is the range of the arccosine, $\R$ the domain of the arctangent.
am1-f-056 · level teorema · notes
Monotonicity and invertibility: which statement is correct for a function $f: D \rr \R$ with any $D \subseteq \R$?
  1. If $f$ is invertible on $D$, then it is strictly monotone on $D$.
  2. If $f$ is strictly increasing on $D$, then it is invertible and its inverse is strictly decreasing.
  3. If $f$ is strictly increasing (decreasing) on $D$, then it is invertible and its inverse is strictly increasing (decreasing).
  4. If $f$ is continuous on $D$, then it is invertible on $D$.
Explanation. Strict monotonicity implies injectivity and the inverse has the same monotonicity. The converse holds only for continuous functions on an interval; $\frac1x$ on $\R\setminus\{0\}$ is invertible but not monotone.
am1-f-057 · level facile · notes
The graph of $f^{-1}$ is obtained from that of $f$ by symmetry with respect to:
  1. the $y$-axis
  2. the line $y = x$
  3. the origin
  4. the $x$-axis
Explanation. The point $(x, y)$ lies on the graph of $f$ if and only if $(y, x)$ lies on the graph of $f^{-1}$: the coordinates are swapped.
am1-f-251 · level facile · notes
What is $\arccos\left(-\frac12\right)$?
  1. $-\frac\pi3$
  2. $\frac\pi3$
  3. $\frac{2\pi}{3}$
  4. $\frac{4\pi}{3}$
Explanation. The arccosine takes values in $[0, \pi]$: the only angle in $[0,\pi]$ with cosine $-\frac12$ is $\frac{2\pi}{3}$.
am1-f-252 · level facile · notes
What is $\arcsin\left(\sin\frac{3\pi}{4}\right)$?
  1. $\frac\pi4$
  2. $\frac{3\pi}{4}$
  3. $-\frac\pi4$
  4. $\frac{5\pi}{4}$
Explanation. $\sin\frac{3\pi}{4} = \frac{\sqrt2}{2}$ and the arcsine returns the angle in $\left[-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right]$ with that sine: $\frac\pi4$. $\arcsin(\sin x) = x$ only for $x \in \left[-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right]$.
am1-f-253 · level facile · notes
What is the inverse of the function $f: [0, \ip) \rr [0, \ip)$, $f(x) = x^4$?
  1. $f^{-1}(y) = y^{-4}$
  2. $f^{-1}(y) = \frac{y}{4}$
  3. $f^{-1}(y) = \pm\sqrt[4]{y}$
  4. $f^{-1}(y) = \sqrt[4]{y}$
Explanation. On $[0, \ip)$ the power $x^4$ is strictly increasing; its inverse is the arithmetic fourth root, with values $\ge 0$ (the $\pm$ sign does not give a function).
am1-f-254 · level facile · notes
What is the image of the arctangent function $\arctan: \R \rr \R$?
  1. $\left[-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right]$
  2. $\left(-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right)$
  3. $(0, \pi)$
  4. $\R$
Explanation. $\arctan$ is the inverse of the tangent restricted to $\left(-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right)$; the values $\pm\frac\pi2$ are not attained (horizontal asymptotes).
am1-f-255 · level facile · notes
What is the inverse of the function $f: \R \rr (1, \ip)$, $f(x) = e^{x} + 1$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $f^{-1}(y) = \log y - 1$
  2. $f^{-1}(y) = \frac{1}{e^y + 1}$
  3. $f^{-1}(y) = \log(y - 1)$
  4. $f^{-1}(y) = e^{y - 1}$
Explanation. From $y = e^x + 1$ we get $e^x = y - 1$, hence $x = \log(y - 1)$, defined exactly for $y > 1$.
am1-f-524 · level facile · notes
Let $f: [0, 2] \rr [1, 5]$ be bijective with $f(2) = 5$, i.e. the point $(2, 5)$ lies on the graph of $f$. Which point lies on the graph of $f^{-1}$?
  1. $(2, 5)$
  2. $(-2, -5)$
  3. $(5, 2)$
  4. $\left(\frac12, \frac15\right)$
Explanation. $f^{-1}(5) = 2$: the graph of $f^{-1}$ is the mirror image of that of $f$ in the line $y = x$, which swaps the coordinates.
am1-f-525 · level facile · notes
On which set must $f(x) = x^2$ be restricted so that it is invertible with inverse $f^{-1}(y) = -\sqrt{y}$, $y \ge 0$?
  1. $[0, \ip)$
  2. $(-\infty, 0]$
  3. $\R$
  4. $[-1, 1]$
Explanation. On $(-\infty, 0]$ the function $x^2$ is strictly decreasing with image $[0, \ip)$, and the solution $x \le 0$ of $x^2 = y$ is $-\sqrt y$.
am1-f-526 · level facile · notes
What is the inverse of the hyperbolic sine $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$, $x \in \R$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $\log\left(y + \sqrt{y^2 - 1}\right)$
  2. $\log\left(y + \sqrt{1 - y^2}\right)$
  3. $\frac{e^y + e^{-y}}{2}$
  4. $\log\left(y + \sqrt{y^2 + 1}\right)$
Explanation. With $t = e^x$ the equation $\frac{t - t^{-1}}{2} = y$ becomes $t^2 - 2yt - 1 = 0$, with positive root $t = y + \sqrt{y^2 + 1}$; hence $x = \log\left(y + \sqrt{y^2 + 1}\right)$, defined for every $y \in \R$.
am1-f-527 · level teorema · notes
The hyperbolic cosine $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$ is not invertible on $\R$. Which restriction makes it invertible, and with which inverse (natural $\log$)?
  1. $[0, \ip)$, with inverse $\log\left(y + \sqrt{y^2 - 1}\right)$, $y \ge 1$
  2. $\R$, with inverse $\log\left(y + \sqrt{y^2 + 1}\right)$, $y \in \R$
  3. $[0, \ip)$, with inverse $\log\left(y + \sqrt{y^2 + 1}\right)$, $y \ge 0$
  4. $[1, \ip)$, with inverse $\log\left(y - \sqrt{y^2 - 1}\right)$, $y \ge 1$
Explanation. $\cosH$ is even, hence not injective on $\R$; on $[0, \ip)$ it is strictly increasing with image $[1, \ip)$. Solving $t^2 - 2yt + 1 = 0$ with $t = e^x \ge 1$ gives $t = y + \sqrt{y^2 - 1}$.

Part: Limits (195)

1. Sequences and limits of sequences (12)

am1-f-058 · level teorema · notes
Which statement is the monotone sequence theorem?
  1. Every bounded sequence is convergent.
  2. Every non-decreasing sequence that is bounded above converges to $\sup\{a_n : n \in \N\}$.
  3. Every non-decreasing sequence that is bounded above converges to $\max\{a_n : n \in \N\}$.
  4. Every convergent sequence is monotone.
Explanation. The limit is the supremum, which need not be a maximum (e.g. $1 - \frac1n$). $(-1)^n$ is bounded but does not converge; $\frac{(-1)^n}{n}$ converges but is not monotone.
am1-f-059 · level facile · notes
The sequence $a_n = (-1)^n$ is:
  1. irregular (indeterminate)
  2. convergent to $0$
  3. divergent to $\ip$
  4. convergent to $1$
Explanation. It alternates between the values $1$ and $-1$: it neither converges nor diverges, so it is irregular.
am1-f-060 · level ipotesi · notes
Which argument proves that a convergent sequence $\{a_n\}$ has only one limit?
  1. the monotone sequence theorem: $\{a_n\}$ has a monotone subsequence, whose limit is the supremum
  2. the comparison theorem applied to the two constant sequences $\ell_1$ and $\ell_2$
  3. the Archimedean property and induction on $n$ in the definition of limit
  4. by contradiction, with two limits $\ell_1 \neq \ell_2$: the triangle inequality gives $|\ell_1 - \ell_2| < 2\varepsilon$ for every $\varepsilon > 0$
Explanation. From $|\ell_1 - \ell_2| \le |a_n - \ell_1| + |a_n - \ell_2| < 2\varepsilon$ for every $\varepsilon$ we get $\ell_1 = \ell_2$: a contradiction.
am1-f-256 · level facile · notes
Definition of limit: $a_n \rr \ell \in \R$ means that:
  1. there is $\varepsilon > 0$ such that $|a_n - \ell| < \varepsilon$ for every $n$
  2. for every $\varepsilon > 0$ we have $|a_n - \ell| < \varepsilon$ for every $n \in \N$
  3. for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $\nu$ such that $|a_n - \ell| < \varepsilon$ for every $n > \nu$
  4. for every $n$ there is $\varepsilon > 0$ such that $|a_n - \ell| < \varepsilon$
Explanation. The distance from $\ell$ must become (eventually) smaller than any fixed $\varepsilon$. The second option asks too much (all $n$), the first and the last too little.
am1-f-257 · level facile · notes
For which $q \in \R$ does the geometric progression $a_n = q^n$ converge?
  1. $-1 < q \le 1$
  2. $|q| < 1$
  3. $|q| \le 1$
  4. $q > -1$
Explanation. $q^n \rr 0$ for $|q| < 1$ and $q^n = 1$ for $q = 1$; for $q > 1$ it diverges to $\ip$, for $q \le -1$ it is irregular (for $q = -1$ it oscillates between $\pm 1$).
am1-f-258 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about convergent sequences is correct?
  1. Every bounded sequence is convergent.
  2. Every convergent sequence is bounded.
  3. Every convergent sequence is monotone.
  4. Every convergent sequence has limit $0$.
Explanation. Eventually $|a_n - \ell| < 1$, and the first terms are finitely many: the sequence is bounded. The converse is false: $(-1)^n$ is bounded but does not converge.
am1-f-259 · level facile · notes
The sequence $a_n = \frac{n}{n + 1}$, $n \ge 0$, is:
  1. decreasing with limit $0$
  2. increasing and divergent
  3. irregular
  4. increasing with limit $1$
Explanation. $a_n = 1 - \frac{1}{n+1}$ increases with $n$ and tends to $1 = \sup\{a_n\}$, which is never attained.
am1-f-260 · level facile · notes
If $a_n \rr \ip$ and $a_n > 0$, the sequence $b_n = \frac{1}{a_n}$:
  1. diverges ($b_n \rr \ip$)
  2. is infinitesimal ($b_n \rr 0$)
  3. converges to $1$ ($b_n \rr 1$)
  4. may be irregular (no limit)
Explanation. The reciprocal of an infinite sequence is infinitesimal: for $\varepsilon > 0$, eventually $a_n > \frac1\varepsilon$, so $0 < b_n < \varepsilon$.
am1-f-528 · level facile · notes
For a non-empty set $E \subseteq \R$ one sets $\sup E = \ip$ if $E$ is not bounded above. What are $\sup E$ and $\inf E$ for $E = \{n^2 - 10n : n \in \N\}$?
  1. $\sup E = \ip$, $\inf E = \min E = -25$
  2. $\sup E = \ip$, $\inf E = \im$
  3. $\sup E = 0$, $\inf E = -25$
  4. $\sup E = \ip$, $\inf E = -24$
Explanation. $n^2 - 10n = (n - 5)^2 - 25 \ge -25$, with equality for $n = 5$: the minimum is $-25$. As $n \rr \ip$ the values grow without bound, so $\sup E = \ip$.
am1-f-529 · level facile · notes
With the convention $\inf E = \im$ for a set that is not bounded below, which statement about $E = \{x \in \R : x^3 < 1\}$ is correct?
  1. $\max E = 1$ and $\inf E = \im$
  2. $\sup E = 1$ and $\inf E = -1$
  3. $\sup E = 1$, which is not a maximum, and $\inf E = \im$
  4. $\sup E = \ip$ and $\inf E = \im$
Explanation. $x^3 < 1 \iff x < 1$, so $E = (-\infty, 1)$: $1$ is the supremum but does not belong to $E$, and $E$ is not bounded below.
am1-f-530 · level facile · notes
Which sequence is infinitesimal (i.e. tends to $0$ as $n \rr \ip$)?
  1. $a_n = (-1)^n$
  2. $a_n = \frac{(-1)^n}{n + 1}$
  3. $a_n = \frac{n}{n + 1}$
  4. $a_n = \log\frac{1}{n + 1}$
Explanation. $\left|\frac{(-1)^n}{n+1}\right| = \frac{1}{n+1} \rr 0$. $(-1)^n$ is irregular, $\frac{n}{n+1} \rr 1$ and $\log\frac{1}{n+1} = -\log(n+1) \rr \im$.
am1-f-531 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \left(-\frac{3}{2}\right)^n$?
  1. $0$
  2. $\ip$
  3. $\im$
  4. it does not exist: the sequence is irregular
Explanation. For the geometric progression $q^n$ with $q < -1$ the terms alternate in sign and have modulus $|q|^n \rr \ip$: there is no limit.

2. Computing limits of sequences (12)

am1-f-061 · level teorema · notes
Which statement is the comparison (squeeze) theorem for sequences?
  1. If $a_n \rr \ell$, $c_n \rr \ell$ with $\ell \in \R$ and $a_n \le b_n \le c_n$ eventually, then $b_n \rr \ell$.
  2. If $a_n \rr \ell$ with $\ell \in \R$ and $a_n \le b_n$ eventually, then $b_n \rr \ell$.
  3. If $a_n \rr \ell_1$, $c_n \rr \ell_2$ and $a_n \le b_n \le c_n$, then $b_n \rr \frac{\ell_1 + \ell_2}{2}$.
  4. If $a_n \le b_n \le c_n$ eventually, then $\{b_n\}$ is convergent.
Explanation. One needs two sequences with the same finite limit squeezing $b_n$. A single lower bound is not enough: $0 \le (-1)^n + 1$ but $(-1)^n + 1$ does not converge.
am1-f-062 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \frac{\sin n}{n}$?
  1. $1$
  2. it does not exist
  3. $0$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. $\{\sin n\}$ is bounded and $\frac1n \rr 0$: the product of a bounded sequence and an infinitesimal one tends to $0$ (corollary of the comparison theorem). The standard limit $\frac{\sin x}{x} \rr 1$ holds as $x \rr 0$, not here.
am1-f-063 · level ipotesi · notes
Sequences $\{c_n\}$ and $\{b_n\}$: which hypotheses suffice to conclude, with the comparison theorem, that $c_n\,b_n \rr 0$?
  1. $c_n \rr 0$ and $b_n \rr \ell \neq 0$
  2. $c_n \rr 0$ and $\{b_n\}$ bounded
  3. $c_n \rr 0$ and $b_n > 0$ for every $n$
  4. $\{c_n\}$ bounded and $b_n \rr \ell \in \R$
Explanation. It is enough that one sequence is infinitesimal and the other bounded. With $c_n \rr 0$ and $b_n = n$ (positive but unbounded) the product need not tend to $0$.
am1-f-261 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \frac{2n^2 - 3n}{5n^2 + 1}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $-3$
  3. $\frac25$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. Factor out $n^2$: $\frac{2 - \frac3n}{5 + \frac1{n^2}} \rr \frac25$. With numerator and denominator of the same degree the ratio of leading coefficients counts.
am1-f-262 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \left(\sqrt{n + 1} - \sqrt{n}\right)$?
  1. $0$
  2. $1$
  3. $\ip$
  4. $\frac12$
Explanation. Form $[\ip - \infty]$: rationalize, $\sqrt{n+1} - \sqrt n = \frac{1}{\sqrt{n+1} + \sqrt n} \rr 0$.
am1-f-263 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \frac{3^n + 2^n}{3^{n+1}}$?
  1. $1$
  2. $0$
  3. $\ip$
  4. $\frac13$
Explanation. $\frac{3^n + 2^n}{3 \cdot 3^n} = \frac13\left(1 + \left(\frac23\right)^n\right) \rr \frac13$, since $\left(\frac23\right)^n \rr 0$.
am1-f-264 · level teorema · notes
Sign preservation theorem for sequences: if $a_n \rr \ell$, which statement is correct?
  1. If $a_n > 0$ for every $n$, then $\ell > 0$.
  2. If $\ell > 0$, then $a_n > 0$ eventually.
  3. If $\ell > 0$, then $a_n > 0$ for every $n$.
  4. If $\ell \ge 0$, then $a_n \ge 0$ eventually.
Explanation. With $\varepsilon = \ell > 0$ we eventually have $a_n > \ell - \varepsilon = 0$. It does not hold for every $n$, and passing to the limit preserves only weak inequalities: $\frac1n > 0$ but the limit is $0$.
am1-f-265 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \left(n^2 - 5n\right)$?
  1. $0$
  2. $\ip$
  3. $\im$
  4. it does not exist
Explanation. Form $[\ip - \infty]$: $n^2 - 5n = n^2\left(1 - \frac5n\right)$, the product of a sequence diverging to $\ip$ and one tending to $1$.
am1-f-532 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \sqrt[n]{5}$?
  1. $1$
  2. $5$
  3. $0$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. $\sqrt[n]{5} = 5^{1/n} = e^{\frac{\log 5}{n}}$ and $\frac{\log 5}{n} \rr 0$, so the limit is $e^0 = 1$.
am1-f-533 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \left(\sqrt{n^2 + 4n} - n\right)$?
  1. $0$
  2. $4$
  3. $2$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. Form $[\ip - \infty]$: rationalise, $\sqrt{n^2 + 4n} - n = \frac{4n}{\sqrt{n^2 + 4n} + n} \rr \frac{4}{1 + 1} = 2$.
am1-f-534 · level facile · notes
Let $a_n \rr 3$ and $b_n \rr \ip$. What are $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \frac{a_n}{b_n}$ and $\lim_{n \rr \ip} a_n\,b_n$?
  1. $0$ and $3$
  2. $0$ and $\ip$
  3. $3$ and $\ip$
  4. they are both indeterminate forms
Explanation. By the algebra of limits $\left[\frac{3}{\ip}\right] = 0$ and $[3 \cdot \ip] = \ip$ (the factor $3$ is positive). Neither is an indeterminate form.
am1-f-535 · level teorema · notes
Algebra of limits for sequences: which statement about the sum $a_n + b_n$ is correct?
  1. If $a_n \rr \ip$ and $b_n \rr \im$, then $a_n + b_n \rr 0$.
  2. If $a_n + b_n$ converges, then $\{a_n\}$ and $\{b_n\}$ converge.
  3. If $a_n \rr \ip$, then $a_n + b_n \rr \ip$ for every sequence $\{b_n\}$.
  4. If $a_n \rr \ip$ and $\{b_n\}$ is bounded below, then $a_n + b_n \rr \ip$.
Explanation. If $b_n \ge m$ for every $n$, then $a_n + b_n \ge a_n + m \rr \ip$. $[\ip - \infty]$ is indeterminate; with $a_n = n$, $b_n = -n$ the sum converges but the terms do not; with $b_n = -2n$ the third fails.

3. Euler's number (12)

am1-f-064 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \left(1 - \frac{1}{n}\right)^n$?
  1. $e$
  2. $\frac{1}{e}$
  3. $1$
  4. $0$
Explanation. We have $\left(1 - \frac{1}{c_n}\right)^{c_n} \rr \frac1e$ for every sequence $c_n \rr \ip$; here $c_n = n$. The answer $1$ comes from the wrong step $1^\infty = 1$.
am1-f-065 · level facile · notes
Unit capital, annual rate $t$, interest paid every month (compounded). What is the capital after one year?
  1. $\left(1 + \frac{t}{12}\right)^{12}$
  2. $1 + t$
  3. $1 + 12\,t$
  4. $(1 + t)^{12}$
Explanation. Every month the capital is multiplied by $1 + \frac{t}{12}$; after $12$ months we get $\left(1 + \frac{t}{12}\right)^{12}$, which tends to $e^t$ as the payments become infinitely many.
am1-f-266 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \left(1 + \frac{1}{n}\right)^{2n}$?
  1. $e$
  2. $2e$
  3. $e^2$
  4. $1$
Explanation. $\left(1 + \frac1n\right)^{2n} = \left[\left(1 + \frac1n\right)^n\right]^2 \rr e^2$. The form $[1^\infty]$ is not $1$.
am1-f-267 · level facile · notes
Euler's number is defined as $e = \lim_{n \rr \ip} \left(1 + \frac1n\right)^n$. Between which integers does it lie?
  1. $2 < e < 3$
  2. $1 < e < 2$
  3. $3 < e < 4$
  4. $e = 2$
Explanation. The sequence $\left(1 + \frac1n\right)^n$ is increasing and equals $2$ for $n = 1$; it is bounded above by $3$. Indeed $e \approx 2.718$.
am1-f-268 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \left(1 + \frac{3}{n}\right)^{n}$?
  1. $3e$
  2. $e^{1/3}$
  3. $1$
  4. $e^3$
Explanation. With $c_n = \frac n3 \rr \ip$: $\left(1 + \frac1{c_n}\right)^{c_n} \rr e$, hence $\left(1 + \frac3n\right)^n = \left[\left(1 + \frac1{c_n}\right)^{c_n}\right]^3 \rr e^3$.
am1-f-269 · level facile · notes
A capital $C$ is invested at the annual rate $t$ with continuous compounding (infinitely many interest payments per year). What is it worth after $2$ years?
  1. $C\,(1 + t)^2$
  2. $C\,e^{2t}$
  3. $C\,e^{t}$
  4. $C\,(1 + 2t)$
Explanation. In one year the capital is multiplied by $\lim_n \left(1 + \frac tn\right)^n = e^t$; in two years by $e^t \cdot e^t = e^{2t}$.
am1-f-270 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the sequence $a_n = \left(1 + \frac1n\right)^n$, $n \ge 1$, is correct?
  1. It is decreasing and converges to $e$.
  2. It is increasing and diverges to $\ip$.
  3. It is increasing, bounded above, and converges to $e$.
  4. It is constant and equal to $1$, since $1 + \frac1n \rr 1$.
Explanation. One proves (with Bernoulli's inequality) that it is increasing and bounded, so it converges by the monotone sequence theorem; the limit is called $e$. Values: $2$, $2.25$, $2.37\ldots$
am1-f-271 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \left(\frac{n}{n + 1}\right)^n$?
  1. $\frac1e$
  2. $e$
  3. $1$
  4. $0$
Explanation. $\left(\frac{n}{n+1}\right)^n = \frac{1}{\left(1 + \frac1n\right)^n} \rr \frac1e$.
am1-f-536 · level teorema · notes
Euler's number is $e = \lim_{n \rr \ip} \left(1 + \frac1n\right)^n$. Which generalisation is correct for a sequence $\{c_n\}$ with $c_n \rr \ip$ or $c_n \rr \im$?
  1. $\left(1 + \frac{1}{c_n}\right)^{c_n} \rr e$
  2. $\left(1 + \frac{1}{c_n}\right)^{c_n} \rr 1$
  3. $\left(1 + \frac{1}{c_n}\right)^{c_n} \rr e$ only if every $c_n$ is an integer
  4. $\left(1 + c_n\right)^{1/c_n} \rr e$
Explanation. The limit is $e$ for any (real) sequence diverging to $\ip$ or to $\im$. Instead $(1 + c_n)^{1/c_n} = e^{\frac{\log(1 + c_n)}{c_n}} \rr 1$ for $c_n \rr \ip$.
am1-f-537 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \left(1 + \frac{2}{n^2}\right)^{n}$?
  1. $e^2$
  2. $e$
  3. $1$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. $\left(1 + \frac{2}{n^2}\right)^{n} = \left[\left(1 + \frac{2}{n^2}\right)^{n^2}\right]^{1/n}$: the base tends to $e^2$ and the exponent $\frac1n$ to $0$, so the limit is $1$.
am1-f-538 · level facile · notes
A capital $C$ is invested at the annual rate $t = 5\%$ with continuous compounding, i.e. it is worth $C\,e^{tT}$ after $T$ years. After how many years does it double ($\log 2 \approx 0.693$)?
  1. $20$ years
  2. about $13.9$ years
  3. about $6.9$ years
  4. about $34.7$ years
Explanation. $e^{0.05\,T} = 2$ gives $T = \frac{\log 2}{0.05} \approx 13.9$. The value $20 = \frac{1}{0.05}$ ignores the logarithm.
am1-f-539 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} n \log\left(1 + \frac1n\right)$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $0$
  2. $e$
  3. $\ip$
  4. $1$
Explanation. $n \log\left(1 + \frac1n\right) = \log\left(1 + \frac1n\right)^n \rr \log e = 1$, by continuity of the logarithm.

4. Comparisons and asymptotic estimates (12)

am1-f-066 · level facile · notes
As $n \rr \ip$, the sequence $a_n = \frac{3n^2 + n}{n + 5}$ is asymptotic to:
  1. $3n^2$
  2. $\frac{n}{5}$
  3. $3$
  4. $3n$
Explanation. Numerator and denominator are estimated factor by factor: $3n^2 + n \sim 3n^2$ and $n + 5 \sim n$, hence $a_n \sim \frac{3n^2}{n} = 3n$.
am1-f-067 · level facile · notes
As $n \rr \ip$ let $a_n \sim a'_n$ and $b_n \sim b'_n$ (where $a_n \sim a'_n$ means $\frac{a_n}{a'_n} \rr 1$). Which statement is always true?
  1. $a_n\,b_n \sim a'_n\,b'_n$
  2. $a_n + b_n \sim a'_n + b'_n$
  3. $a_n - a'_n \rr 0$
  4. $e^{a_n} \sim e^{a'_n}$
Explanation. Asymptotic estimates can be made factor by factor in products and quotients, not in sums nor in exponents: $n^2 + n \sim n^2$ but the difference is $n$ and $e^{n^2+n}/e^{n^2} = e^n$.
am1-f-272 · level facile · notes
Two sequences are asymptotic, $a_n \sim b_n$, if $\frac{a_n}{b_n} \rr 1$. Which pair is NOT asymptotic as $n \rr \ip$?
  1. $n^2 + n$ and $n^2$
  2. $2^{n+1}$ and $2^n$
  3. $\sqrt{n^2 + 1}$ and $n$
  4. $\log(n^2)$ and $2\log n$
Explanation. $\frac{2^{n+1}}{2^n} = 2 \not\rr 1$. In the other cases the ratio tends to $1$ (in the last one it is exactly $1$).
am1-f-273 · level facile · notes
As $n \rr \ip$, the sequence $a_n = \frac{\sqrt{4n^2 + 1}}{n + 3}$ is asymptotic to:
  1. $4$
  2. $\frac{4n}{3}$
  3. $1$
  4. $2$
Explanation. $\sqrt{4n^2 + 1} \sim 2n$ and $n + 3 \sim n$, so $a_n \sim \frac{2n}{n} = 2$.
am1-f-274 · level facile · notes
As $n \rr \ip$ we have $\sin\frac1n \sim \frac1n$. What is $a_n = n^2 \sin\frac{3}{n^2}$ asymptotic to?
  1. $3$
  2. $1$
  3. $3n^2$
  4. $0$
Explanation. $\frac{3}{n^2} \rr 0$, so $\sin\frac{3}{n^2} \sim \frac{3}{n^2}$ and $a_n \sim n^2 \cdot \frac{3}{n^2} = 3$.
am1-f-275 · level teorema · notes
If $a_n \sim b_n$ as $n \rr \ip$ (i.e. $\frac{a_n}{b_n} \rr 1$) and $b_n \rr \ell \in \R \cup \{\pm\infty\}$, which statement is correct?
  1. $a_n - b_n \rr 0$
  2. $a_n \rr 1$
  3. $a_n \rr \ell$
  4. $a_n = b_n$ eventually
Explanation. $a_n = \frac{a_n}{b_n} \cdot b_n$ and the first factor tends to $1$: asymptotic sequences have the same limit. The difference need not tend to $0$: $n^2 + n \sim n^2$.
am1-f-276 · level facile · notes
Using asymptotic estimates, what is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \frac{n \log\left(1 + \frac2n\right)}{1 + \frac1n}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $2$
  3. $1$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. $\log\left(1 + \frac2n\right) \sim \frac2n$, so the numerator is asymptotic to $2$, while the denominator tends to $1$.
am1-f-277 · level facile · notes
As $n \rr \ip$, what is $a_n = n^3 - 2n^2 + 7$ asymptotic to?
  1. $-2n^2$
  2. $7$
  3. $n^3$
  4. $n^3 - 2n^2$, but not to $n^3$
Explanation. $\frac{n^3 - 2n^2 + 7}{n^3} = 1 - \frac2n + \frac7{n^3} \rr 1$: a polynomial is asymptotic to its leading term.
am1-f-540 · level teorema · notes
Substitution principle: let $a_n \sim a'_n$ and $b_n \sim b'_n$ as $n \rr \ip$ (i.e. $\frac{a_n}{a'_n} \rr 1$ and $\frac{b_n}{b'_n} \rr 1$). Which equality of limits is guaranteed, if one side exists?
  1. $\lim (a_n - b_n) = \lim (a'_n - b'_n)$
  2. $\lim \frac{a_n}{b_n} = \lim \frac{a'_n}{b'_n}$
  3. $\lim (a_n + b_n) = \lim (a'_n + b'_n)$
  4. $\lim \left(a_n - a'_n\right) = 0$
Explanation. $\frac{a_n}{b_n} = \frac{a'_n}{b'_n} \cdot \frac{a_n}{a'_n} \cdot \frac{b'_n}{b_n}$ and the last two factors tend to $1$. In sums and differences substitution is not allowed: $n + 1 \sim n$ but $(n + 1) - n \not\rr 0$.
am1-f-541 · level facile · notes
As $n \rr \ip$, what is the sequence $a_n = \frac{1}{n} - \frac{1}{n + 1}$ asymptotic to?
  1. $\frac{1}{n^2}$
  2. $0$
  3. $\frac{1}{n}$
  4. $\frac{2}{n}$
Explanation. $a_n = \frac{1}{n(n + 1)}$ and $\frac{a_n}{1/n^2} = \frac{n}{n + 1} \rr 1$. No sequence is asymptotic to $0$ (the ratio is undefined).
am1-f-542 · level facile · notes
Which asymptotic estimate is correct as $n \rr \ip$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $e^{n + 1} \sim e^n$
  2. $(n + 1)^n \sim n^n$
  3. $\log(n^3 + n) \sim 3\log n$
  4. $\sqrt{n + 1} - \sqrt{n} \sim 1$
Explanation. $\log(n^3 + n) = 3\log n + \log\left(1 + \frac{1}{n^2}\right)$ and the ratio with $3\log n$ tends to $1$. Instead $\frac{e^{n+1}}{e^n} = e$, $\frac{(n+1)^n}{n^n} \rr e$ and $\sqrt{n+1} - \sqrt n \rr 0$.
am1-f-543 · level facile · notes
As $n \rr \ip$, what is $a_n = \frac{2^n + n^3}{3^n}$ asymptotic to?
  1. $\frac{n^3}{3^n}$
  2. $\frac{1}{3^n}$
  3. $\frac{2}{3}$
  4. $\left(\frac23\right)^n$
Explanation. $n^3 = o(2^n)$, so $2^n + n^3 \sim 2^n$ and $a_n \sim \frac{2^n}{3^n} = \left(\frac23\right)^n$.

5. Hierarchies of infinities and the ratio test (12)

am1-f-068 · level teorema · notes
Ratio test for sequences: let $a_n > 0$ and $\frac{a_{n+1}}{a_n} \rr l$. Which statement is correct?
  1. If $l < 1$, then $a_n \rr l$.
  2. If $l = 1$, then $a_n \rr 1$.
  3. If $l < 1$, then $a_n \rr 0$; if $l > 1$ (or $l = \ip$), then $a_n \rr \ip$.
  4. If $l < 1$, then $a_n \rr \ip$; if $l > 1$, then $a_n \rr 0$.
Explanation. The test compares $a_n$ with a geometric progression with ratio close to $l$. With $l = 1$ it says nothing: $a_n = n$ and $a_n = \frac1n$ both have $l = 1$.
am1-f-069 · level facile · notes
Hierarchy of infinities as $n \rr \ip$ (each term negligible with respect to the next):
  1. $\log n,~ \sqrt n,~ 2^n,~ n!,~ n^n$
  2. $\log n,~ \sqrt n,~ n!,~ 2^n,~ n^n$
  3. $\sqrt n,~ \log n,~ 2^n,~ n!,~ n^n$
  4. $\log n,~ \sqrt n,~ 2^n,~ n^n,~ n!$
Explanation. For $a > 1$ and $\alpha > 0$: $\frac{\log_a n}{n^\alpha} \rr 0$, $\frac{n^\alpha}{a^n} \rr 0$, $\frac{a^n}{n!} \rr 0$, $\frac{n!}{n^n} \rr 0$.
am1-f-070 · level ipotesi · notes
Hierarchy of infinities: how does one prove that $\frac{a^n}{n!} \rr 0$ as $n \rr \ip$, for every $a > 0$?
  1. with Stirling's formula $n! \sim \sqrt{2\pi n}\,\left(\frac ne\right)^n$
  2. with the comparison theorem, using $\frac{a^n}{n!} \le \frac1n$ for every $n \ge 1$
  3. with L'Hôpital's rule applied to $\frac{a^x}{x!}$
  4. with the ratio test for $b_n = \frac{a^n}{n!}$: $\frac{b_{n+1}}{b_n} = \frac{a}{n+1} \rr 0$
Explanation. The ratio $\frac{b_{n+1}}{b_n} = \frac{a}{n+1}$ tends to $0 < 1$, so by the ratio test $b_n \rr 0$. The same scheme works for $\frac{n!}{n^n}$.
am1-f-278 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \frac{n^5}{2^n}$?
  1. $\ip$
  2. $1$
  3. $0$
  4. $5$
Explanation. Hierarchy of infinities: $\frac{n^\alpha}{a^n} \rr 0$ for every $\alpha > 0$ and $a > 1$; the exponential beats every power.
am1-f-279 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \frac{n! + 3^n}{n^n}$?
  1. $\ip$
  2. $0$
  3. $1$
  4. $\frac13$
Explanation. $\frac{n!}{n^n} \rr 0$ and $\frac{3^n}{n^n} = \left(\frac3n\right)^n \rr 0$: in the hierarchy $n^n$ is the infinity of highest order.
am1-f-280 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \frac{(\log n)^{10}}{\sqrt n}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $\ip$
  3. $10$
  4. $1$
Explanation. Every power of the logarithm is negligible with respect to every positive power of $n$: $\frac{(\log n)^\beta}{n^\alpha} \rr 0$ for $\alpha, \beta > 0$.
am1-f-281 · level facile · notes
Ratio test for the sequence $a_n = \frac{n^2}{3^n}$: what is $\lim \frac{a_{n+1}}{a_n}$ and what follows?
  1. $3$, so $a_n \rr \ip$
  2. $1$, so the test is inconclusive
  3. $\frac13$, so $a_n \rr \frac13$
  4. $\frac13$, so $a_n \rr 0$
Explanation. $\frac{a_{n+1}}{a_n} = \frac{(n+1)^2}{n^2} \cdot \frac13 \rr \frac13 < 1$, hence $a_n \rr 0$ (the limit of the ratio is not the limit of $a_n$).
am1-f-282 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \frac{2^n + n^{100}}{2^n + \log n}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $1$
  3. $\ip$
  4. $\frac12$
Explanation. In both numerator and denominator $2^n$ dominates ($n^{100}$ and $\log n$ are negligible): the quotient is asymptotic to $\frac{2^n}{2^n} = 1$.
am1-f-544 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \frac{n^{3} + 2^{n}}{n^{4} + 1}$?
  1. $\ip$
  2. $0$
  3. $1$
  4. $\frac12$
Explanation. In the hierarchy of infinities $n^4 = o(2^n)$: the numerator is asymptotic to $2^n$ and $\frac{2^n}{n^4} \rr \ip$.
am1-f-545 · level facile · notes
Which sequence tends to $\ip$ fastest, in the sense that the other three are negligible with respect to it as $n \rr \ip$?
  1. $10^n$
  2. $n^{100}$
  3. $n!$
  4. $(\log n)^{1000}$
Explanation. In the hierarchy $(\log n)^\beta = o(n^\alpha)$, $n^\alpha = o(a^n)$, $a^n = o(n!)$ (for $\alpha, \beta > 0$, $a > 1$): $n!$ beats even $10^n$.
am1-f-546 · level facile · notes
Ratio test for the positive sequence $a_n = \frac{n!}{2^n}$: what is obtained?
  1. $\frac{a_{n+1}}{a_n} \rr \frac12$, hence $a_n \rr 0$
  2. $\frac{a_{n+1}}{a_n} \rr 1$, hence the test is inconclusive
  3. $\frac{a_{n+1}}{a_n} \rr 2$, hence $a_n \rr \ip$
  4. $\frac{a_{n+1}}{a_n} \rr \ip$, hence $a_n \rr \ip$
Explanation. $\frac{a_{n+1}}{a_n} = \frac{(n+1)!}{2^{n+1}} \cdot \frac{2^n}{n!} = \frac{n + 1}{2} \rr \ip > 1$, hence $a_n \rr \ip$.
am1-f-547 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \frac{n^{2}}{(\log n)^{5} + \sqrt{n}}$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $0$
  2. $\ip$
  3. $1$
  4. $5$
Explanation. $(\log n)^5 = o(\sqrt n)$, so the denominator is asymptotic to $\sqrt n$ and the sequence to $n^{3/2} \rr \ip$.

6. Recursively defined sequences (12)

am1-f-071 · level facile · notes
Heron's algorithm for $\sqrt2$: $a_0 = 2$, $a_{n+1} = \frac12\left(a_n + \frac{2}{a_n}\right)$. What is $a_2$?
  1. $\frac32$
  2. $\frac{17}{12}$
  3. $\frac75$
  4. $\frac43$
Explanation. $a_1 = \frac12(2 + 1) = \frac32$, $a_2 = \frac12\left(\frac32 + \frac43\right) = \frac{17}{12} \approx 1.4167$.
am1-f-072 · level facile · notes
Let $a_0 = b > 0$, $a_{n+1} = \frac{a_n}{1 + a_n}$. The sequence is positive and decreasing; what is its limit?
  1. $0$
  2. $1$
  3. $b$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. It converges by the monotone sequence theorem; the limit satisfies $\ell = \frac{\ell}{1 + \ell}$, hence $\ell = 0$.
am1-f-283 · level facile · notes
Let $a_0 = 1$ and $a_{n+1} = 2a_n + 1$. What is $a_4$?
  1. $15$
  2. $9$
  3. $31$
  4. $16$
Explanation. $a_1 = 3$, $a_2 = 7$, $a_3 = 15$, $a_4 = 31$. In closed form $a_n = 2^{n+1} - 1$.
am1-f-284 · level facile · notes
Let $a_0 = 1$, $a_{n+1} = \sqrt{2 + a_n}$. The sequence is increasing and bounded; what is its limit?
  1. $2$
  2. $\sqrt2$
  3. $-1$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. The limit $\ell$ satisfies $\ell = \sqrt{2 + \ell}$, i.e. $\ell^2 - \ell - 2 = 0$, with solutions $2$ and $-1$; $-1$ is rejected because the terms are positive.
am1-f-285 · level ipotesi · notes
Recursive sequence $a_{n+1} = f(a_n)$: we want to conclude that its limit $\ell$ satisfies $\ell = f(\ell)$. Which facts are needed?
  1. one needs convergence of $\{a_n\}$ and monotonicity of $f$
  2. one needs monotonicity of $\{a_n\}$ and boundedness of $f$
  3. one needs $a_0 > 0$ and differentiability of $f$ at $a_0$
  4. one needs convergence of $\{a_n\}$ to $\ell \in \R$ and continuity of $f$ at $\ell$
Explanation. Passing to the limit in $a_{n+1} = f(a_n)$: the left side tends to $\ell$ and, by continuity, the right side to $f(\ell)$. Without knowing that $\{a_n\}$ converges, $\ell = f(\ell)$ says nothing (with $a_{n+1} = 2a_n$, $a_0 = 1$, we would get $\ell = 0$).
am1-f-286 · level facile · notes
Heron's algorithm for $\sqrt{b}$: $a_{n+1} = \frac12\left(a_n + \frac{b}{a_n}\right)$. With $b = 9$ and $a_0 = 1$, what is $a_1$?
  1. $3$
  2. $5$
  3. $\frac{9}{2}$
  4. $10$
Explanation. $a_1 = \frac12\left(1 + \frac91\right) = 5$. The next terms ($a_2 = 3.4$, $a_3 \approx 3.02$) quickly approach $3$.
am1-f-287 · level facile · notes
In Heron's algorithm for $\sqrt b$ ($a_{n+1} = \frac12\left(a_n + \frac b{a_n}\right)$, $a_0 > 0$), from $n = 1$ on the terms satisfy:
  1. $a_n < \sqrt b$ and the sequence is increasing
  2. $a_n$ alternately above and below $\sqrt b$
  3. $a_n \ge \sqrt b$ and the sequence is decreasing
  4. $a_n = \sqrt b$
Explanation. By the arithmetic-geometric mean inequality $a_{n+1} = \frac12\left(a_n + \frac{b}{a_n}\right) \ge \sqrt{a_n \cdot \frac b{a_n}} = \sqrt b$; then $a_{n+1} \le a_n$ because $\frac b{a_n} \le a_n$.
am1-f-288 · level facile · notes
Let $a_0 = 4$ and $a_{n+1} = \frac{a_n}{2} + 1$. What is the limit of the sequence?
  1. $0$
  2. $4$
  3. $1$
  4. $2$
Explanation. The sequence is monotone and bounded (between $2$ and $4$); the limit satisfies $\ell = \frac\ell2 + 1$, i.e. $\ell = 2$. Indeed $a_n - 2 = \frac{2}{2^n}$.
am1-f-548 · level facile · notes
Let $a_1 = 1$ and $a_{n+1} = a_n + 2n + 1$ for $n \ge 1$. What is $a_5$?
  1. $25$
  2. $16$
  3. $36$
  4. $21$
Explanation. $a_2 = 1 + 3 = 4$, $a_3 = 4 + 5 = 9$, $a_4 = 9 + 7 = 16$, $a_5 = 16 + 9 = 25$: indeed $a_n = n^2$.
am1-f-549 · level facile · notes
Let $a_0 = 3$ and $a_{n+1} = \frac{a_n}{3}$ for $n \ge 0$. What is the explicit expression of $a_n$?
  1. $a_n = 3^{-n}$
  2. $a_n = \frac{3}{n + 1}$
  3. $a_n = 3^{1 - n}$
  4. $a_n = 3 - n$
Explanation. It is a geometric progression with ratio $\frac13$ and first term $3$: $a_n = 3 \cdot 3^{-n} = 3^{1 - n}$ (check: $a_0 = 3$, $a_1 = 1$).
am1-f-550 · level facile · notes
Heron's algorithm for $\sqrt{b}$ ($b > 0$): $a_{n+1} = \frac12\left(a_n + \frac{b}{a_n}\right)$ with $a_0 > 0$. Knowing that $a_n \rr \ell > 0$, which equation does $\ell$ satisfy and what is it?
  1. $\ell = \frac{\ell}{2}$, hence $\ell = 0$
  2. $\ell = \frac12\left(\ell + \frac{b}{\ell}\right)$, hence $\ell = \sqrt b$
  3. $\ell = \frac12\left(\ell + \frac{b}{\ell}\right)$, hence $\ell = \frac b2$
  4. $\ell^2 = 2b$, hence $\ell = \sqrt{2b}$
Explanation. Passing to the limit in the recurrence (the function $x \mt \frac12\left(x + \frac bx\right)$ is continuous for $x > 0$): $2\ell = \ell + \frac b\ell$, i.e. $\ell^2 = b$.
am1-f-551 · level facile · notes
Let $a_0 = 0$ and $a_{n+1} = \frac{a_n^2 + 1}{2}$. The sequence is increasing and bounded above by $1$; what is its limit?
  1. $\frac12$
  2. $0$
  3. $\ip$
  4. $1$
Explanation. The limit $\ell$ exists (monotone bounded sequence) and satisfies $\ell = \frac{\ell^2 + 1}{2}$, i.e. $(\ell - 1)^2 = 0$: $\ell = 1$.

1. Limits of functions, asymptotes and continuity (15)

am1-f-073 · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = x + 1$ for $x < 0$ and $f(x) = x^2 + 3$ for $x \ge 0$. What is the jump at $c = 0$, i.e. right limit minus left limit?
  1. $2$
  2. $-2$
  3. $3$
  4. $1$
Explanation. Jump $= \lim_{x \rr 0^+} f(x) - \lim_{x \rr 0^-} f(x) = 3 - 1 = 2$ (right limit minus left limit).
am1-f-074 · level facile · notes
What is the oblique asymptote of $f(x) = \frac{2x^2 + 3x}{x + 1}$ as $x \rr \pm\infty$?
  1. $y = 2x + 3$
  2. $y = 2x$
  3. $y = 2x + 1$
  4. $y = x + 1$
Explanation. $m = \lim \frac{f(x)}{x} = 2$, $q = \lim (f(x) - 2x) = \lim \frac{x}{x+1} = 1$; indeed $f(x) = 2x + 1 - \frac{1}{x+1}$.
am1-f-075 · level facile · notes
Which vertical asymptotes does $f(x) = \frac{x + 2}{x^2 - 9}$ have?
  1. only $x = 3$
  2. $x = 3$ and $x = -3$
  3. $x = -2$
  4. none: it only has the horizontal asymptote $y = 0$
Explanation. The denominator vanishes at $x = \pm 3$ where the numerator does not, so $f \rr \pm\infty$. At $x = -2$ the function equals $0$. The horizontal asymptote $y = 0$ exists, but it does not exclude vertical ones.
am1-f-289 · level facile · notes
What are the limits of $f(x) = \frac{1}{x - 2}$ as $x \rr 2^-$ and as $x \rr 2^+$?
  1. $\ip$ and $\im$
  2. $\im$ and $\ip$
  3. $\ip$ and $\ip$
  4. $0$ and $0$
Explanation. As $x \rr 2^-$ the denominator is negative and tends to $0$: $f \rr \im$; as $x \rr 2^+$ it is positive: $f \rr \ip$. The line $x = 2$ is a vertical asymptote.
am1-f-290 · level facile · notes
What is the horizontal asymptote of $f(x) = \frac{3x - 1}{x + 4}$ as $x \rr \pm\infty$?
  1. $y = -\frac14$
  2. $y = 0$
  3. $x = -4$
  4. $y = 3$
Explanation. $\frac{3x - 1}{x + 4} = \frac{3 - \frac1x}{1 + \frac4x} \rr 3$. The line $x = -4$ is the vertical asymptote instead.
am1-f-291 · level facile · notes
The limit $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \sin x$:
  1. does not exist: along $x_n = n\pi$ and $x_n = \frac\pi2 + 2n\pi$ the limits of $\sin x_n$ differ
  2. equals $0$: along $x_n = n\pi$ we have $\sin x_n = 0$ for every $n$
  3. equals $1$: $\sin x \le 1$ and the value $1$ is taken infinitely often
  4. equals $\frac12$: the mean of the limits along $n\pi$ and $\frac\pi2 + 2n\pi$
Explanation. With the sequential definition: $\sin(n\pi) = 0 \rr 0$ but $\sin\left(\frac\pi2 + 2n\pi\right) = 1 \rr 1$. Two sequences $x_n \rr \ip$ give different limits.
am1-f-292 · level facile · notes
Sequential definition: $\lim_{x \rr x_0} f(x) = \ell$ means that:
  1. $f(x_n) \rr \ell$ for at least one sequence $x_n \rr x_0$ with $x_n \neq x_0$
  2. $f(x_0) = \ell$
  3. $f(x_n) \rr \ell$ for every sequence $x_n \rr x_0$ with $x_n \neq x_0$ in the domain
  4. $f(x) = \ell$ in a neighborhood of $x_0$
Explanation. The limit must be the same along all sequences tending to $x_0$ (without taking the value $x_0$). The value $f(x_0)$ does not matter.
am1-f-293 · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = \frac{x^2 - 1}{x - 1}$ for $x \neq 1$ and $f(1) = 3$. At $x = 1$ the function:
  1. is continuous, because the limit as $x \rr 1$ is $3$
  2. has limit $2$ but is not continuous (removable discontinuity)
  3. has a jump, because the right and left limits differ
  4. has a vertical asymptote, because the denominator vanishes
Explanation. For $x \neq 1$, $f(x) = x + 1 \rr 2$; since $f(1) = 3 \neq 2$, $f$ is not continuous at $1$, but redefining $f(1) = 2$ makes it so.
am1-f-552 · level facile · notes
The neighborhood with centre $x_0 \in \R$ and radius $r > 0$ is the set:
  1. $(x_0 - r, x_0 + r)$
  2. $[x_0, x_0 + r)$
  3. $(x_0 - r, x_0)$
  4. $[x_0 - r, x_0 + r] \setminus \{x_0\}$
Explanation. It is the open interval of the points at distance less than $r$ from $x_0$: $\{x : |x - x_0| < r\}$. The second and third are right and left (one-sided) neighborhoods.
am1-f-553 · level facile · notes
Topological definition of limit: with $x_0$ an accumulation point of the domain of $f$, $\lim_{x \rr x_0} f(x) = \ell$ means that:
  1. for every neighborhood $U$ of $x_0$ there is a neighborhood $V$ of $\ell$ with $f(x) \in V$ for every $x \in U$ in the domain, $x \neq x_0$
  2. there are a neighborhood $V$ of $\ell$ and a neighborhood $U$ of $x_0$ with $f(x) \in V$ for every $x \in U$ in the domain, $x \neq x_0$
  3. for every neighborhood $V$ of $\ell$ there is a neighborhood $U$ of $x_0$ with $f(x) \in V$ for every $x \in U$ in the domain, including $x_0$
  4. for every neighborhood $V$ of $\ell$ there is a neighborhood $U$ of $x_0$ with $f(x) \in V$ for every $x \in U$ in the domain, $x \neq x_0$
Explanation. The neighborhood $V$ of the limit is arbitrary (as small as we like) and the neighborhood $U$ depends on $V$. The value at $x_0$ (if any) does not matter.
am1-f-554 · level facile · notes
Definition: $\lim_{x \rr \ip} f(x) = \ell \in \R$ means that:
  1. there is $\varepsilon > 0$ such that $|f(x) - \ell| < \varepsilon$ for every $x$ in the domain
  2. for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $M$ such that $|f(x) - \ell| < \varepsilon$ for every $x > M$ in the domain
  3. for every $M$ there is $\varepsilon > 0$ such that $|f(x) - \ell| < \varepsilon$ for every $x > M$ in the domain
  4. for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $M$ such that $|f(x) - \ell| < \varepsilon$ for every $x < M$ in the domain
Explanation. Given an arbitrary tolerance $\varepsilon$, from some point $M$ on the values of $f$ are within $\varepsilon$ of $\ell$. The third condition is satisfied by every bounded function.
am1-f-555 · level facile · notes
Definition: $\lim_{x \rr \ip} f(x) = \ip$ means that:
  1. there are $K > 0$ and $M$ such that $f(x) > K$ for every $x > M$ in the domain
  2. for every $K > 0$ there is $x > K$ in the domain with $f(x) > K$
  3. for every $K > 0$ there is $M$ such that $f(x) > K$ for every $x > M$ in the domain
  4. $f$ is increasing and not bounded above
Explanation. $f$ eventually exceeds every threshold $K$. A function like $x \sin x$ satisfies the second condition without tending to $\ip$; the fourth is not necessary (nor sufficient if the domain is bounded).
am1-f-556 · level facile · notes
$\varepsilon$–$\delta$ definition of $\lim_{x \rr 2} (3x - 1) = 5$: given $\varepsilon > 0$, which $\delta > 0$ guarantees $|(3x - 1) - 5| < \varepsilon$ for every $x$ with $|x - 2| < \delta$?
  1. $\delta = 3\varepsilon$
  2. $\delta = \varepsilon$
  3. $\delta = \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$
  4. $\delta = \frac{\varepsilon}{3}$
Explanation. $|(3x - 1) - 5| = 3|x - 2| < 3\delta$: it suffices that $3\delta \le \varepsilon$. With $\delta = \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$, $\varepsilon$ or $3\varepsilon$ one only gets $3|x - 2| < \frac{3\varepsilon}{2}$ (or worse).
am1-f-557 · level teorema · notes
Uniqueness theorem for limits: if $\lim_{x \rr x_0} f(x) = \ell_1$ and $\lim_{x \rr x_0} f(x) = \ell_2$, with $\ell_1, \ell_2 \in \R$, what is concluded?
  1. $\ell_1 = \ell_2$
  2. $|\ell_1 - \ell_2| < 1$
  3. $\ell_1 = \ell_2$ only if $f$ is continuous at $x_0$
  4. $\ell_1 = \ell_2$ only if $f(x_0)$ is defined
Explanation. If $\ell_1 \neq \ell_2$, disjoint neighborhoods of $\ell_1$ and $\ell_2$ would both have to contain $f(x)$ for $x$ near $x_0$: a contradiction. The value $f(x_0)$ plays no role.
am1-f-558 · level teorema · notes
Let $x_0$ be an accumulation point of the domain of $f$. Which condition guarantees that $\lim_{x \rr x_0} f(x)$ does NOT exist?
  1. there is a sequence $x_n \rr x_0$ with $f(x_n)$ converging to a value different from $f(x_0)$
  2. there are two sequences $x_n \rr x_0$ and $y_n \rr x_0$, with terms different from $x_0$, such that $f(x_n)$ and $f(y_n)$ have different limits
  3. $f$ is not defined at $x_0$
  4. $f$ is not monotone in any neighborhood of $x_0$
Explanation. By the sequential definition, if the limit existed every $f(x_n)$ with $x_n \rr x_0$, $x_n \neq x_0$ would have the same limit. The first condition only indicates a discontinuity; $x \sin\frac1x$ at $0$ disproves the fourth.

2. Computing limits of functions (12)

am1-f-076 · level facile · notes
Which of the following is NOT an indeterminate form?
  1. $[\ell \cdot \infty]$ with $\ell \neq 0$
  2. $[\ip - \infty]$
  3. $[0 \cdot \infty]$
  4. $\left[\frac{\infty}{\infty}\right]$
Explanation. By the partial arithmetization theorem (product), if $f \rr \ell \neq 0$ and $h \rr \infty$ then $f\,h \rr \infty$. The other three are indeterminate forms.
am1-f-077 · level ipotesi · notes
Change of variable theorem: to conclude $\lim_{x \rr x_0} f\big(g(x)\big) = \lim_{t \rr t_0} f(t)$, which hypotheses are needed?
  1. $g(x) \rr t_0$ and $f(t) \rr \ell$ as $t \rr t_0$
  2. $g(x) \rr t_0$, $f(t) \rr \ell$ as $t \rr t_0$ and $g$ differentiable at $x_0$
  3. $g(x) \rr t_0$, $f(t) \rr \ell$ as $t \rr t_0$ and $f(t_0) = \ell$
  4. $g(x) \rr t_0$, $f(t) \rr \ell$ as $t \rr t_0$ and $g(x) \neq t_0$ near $x_0$
Explanation. One needs $g(x) \neq t_0$ for $x \neq x_0$ in a neighborhood of $x_0$; without this condition the theorem may fail: with $g \equiv t_0$ the composite is constantly $f(t_0)$, which may differ from $\ell$.
am1-f-078 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 0} x \sin\frac{1}{x}$?
  1. $1$
  2. $0$
  3. it does not exist
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. $\left|x\sin\frac1x\right| \le |x| \rr 0$: by the corollary of the comparison theorem the limit is $0$, even though $\sin\frac1x$ has no limit.
am1-f-294 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 2} \frac{x^2 + 1}{x + 3}$?
  1. $1$
  2. $\frac15$
  3. $\frac{4}{5}$
  4. $0$
Explanation. Numerator and denominator are continuous and the denominator does not vanish at $2$: substitute, $\frac{4 + 1}{2 + 3} = 1$.
am1-f-295 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \frac{\cos x}{x}$?
  1. $1$
  2. it does not exist
  3. $0$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. $\left|\frac{\cos x}{x}\right| \le \frac{1}{x} \rr 0$: by the comparison theorem the limit is $0$, although $\cos x$ has no limit.
am1-f-296 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 0^+} \frac{2 + x}{x}$?
  1. $2$
  2. $0$
  3. $\im$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. The numerator tends to $2 > 0$ and the denominator to $0^+$: by partial arithmetization $\left[\frac{2}{0^+}\right] = \ip$.
am1-f-297 · level teorema · notes
Sign preservation theorem for functions: if $\lim_{x \rr x_0} f(x) = \ell$, which statement is correct?
  1. If $f(x) > 0$ near $x_0$, then $\ell > 0$.
  2. If $\ell > 0$, then $f(x) > 0$ in a neighborhood of $x_0$ (except possibly at $x_0$).
  3. If $\ell > 0$, then $f(x) > 0$ for every $x$ in the domain.
  4. If $\ell = 0$, then $f(x) = 0$ in a neighborhood of $x_0$.
Explanation. With $\varepsilon = \ell$ the definition of limit gives $f(x) > \ell - \varepsilon = 0$ near $x_0$. The converse holds only with $\ge$: $x^2 > 0$ for $x \neq 0$ but the limit at $0$ is $0$.
am1-f-298 · level facile · notes
With the change of variable $t = \frac1x$, what is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} x \sin\frac1x$?
  1. $0$
  2. $1$
  3. $\ip$
  4. it does not exist
Explanation. As $x \rr \ip$ we have $t = \frac1x \rr 0^+$ and $x\sin\frac1x = \frac{\sin t}{t} \rr 1$.
am1-f-559 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 1} \frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $1$
  3. $3$
  4. it does not exist
Explanation. Form $\left[\frac00\right]$: $x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)$, so the ratio equals $x^2 + x + 1 \rr 3$.
am1-f-560 · level facile · notes
If $f(x) \rr 0^+$ as $x \rr x_0$ (i.e. $f(x) \rr 0$ with $f(x) > 0$ near $x_0$), what is $\lim_{x \rr x_0} \frac{1}{f(x)}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $\im$
  3. it does not exist in general
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. The reciprocal of a positive quantity tending to $0$ diverges to $\ip$: $\left[\frac{1}{0^+}\right] = \ip$. Without information on the sign the limit might not exist.
am1-f-561 · level facile · notes
With the change of variable $t = x - 1$, what is $\lim_{x \rr 1} \frac{\sin(x - 1)}{x^2 - 1}$?
  1. $\frac12$
  2. $1$
  3. $0$
  4. $2$
Explanation. With $t = x - 1 \rr 0$ we have $x^2 - 1 = t(t + 2)$, so the limit is $\lim_{t \rr 0} \frac{\sin t}{t} \cdot \frac{1}{t + 2} = 1 \cdot \frac12$.
am1-f-562 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 0^-} e^{1/x}$?
  1. $\ip$
  2. $0$
  3. $1$
  4. it does not exist
Explanation. As $x \rr 0^-$ we have $\frac1x \rr \im$ and hence $e^{1/x} \rr 0$. From the right instead $e^{1/x} \rr \ip$: the two-sided limit does not exist, but the left one does.

3. Limits of polynomials and rational functions (12)

am1-f-079 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \im} \left(-2x^3 + x^2 + 5\right)$?
  1. $\ip$
  2. $\im$
  3. $-2$
  4. $0$
Explanation. The polynomial behaves like its highest-degree term: $-2x^3 \rr -2 \cdot (\im) = \ip$ as $x \rr \im$.
am1-f-080 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \frac{x^{2/3} + 1}{x^{1/2} + 2}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $1$
  3. $\frac12$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. Only the powers with the largest exponent matter: $\frac{x^{2/3}}{x^{1/2}} = x^{1/6} \rr \ip$, since $\frac23 > \frac12$.
am1-f-299 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \frac{x^3 + 1}{2x^2 - x}$?
  1. $\frac12$
  2. $\ip$
  3. $0$
  4. $\im$
Explanation. The leading terms count: $\frac{x^3}{2x^2} = \frac x2 \rr \ip$. The degree of the numerator exceeds that of the denominator.
am1-f-300 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \im} \frac{4x^2 + x}{1 - 2x^3}$?
  1. $-2$
  2. $\ip$
  3. $0$
  4. $\im$
Explanation. $\frac{4x^2}{-2x^3} = -\frac{2}{x} \rr 0$ as $x \rr \im$: the degree of the denominator is larger.
am1-f-301 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \im} \left(x^4 - 3x^5\right)$?
  1. $\ip$
  2. $\im$
  3. $0$
  4. $-3$
Explanation. The polynomial behaves like $-3x^5$: as $x \rr \im$, $x^5 \rr \im$ and hence $-3x^5 \rr \ip$.
am1-f-302 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \frac{\sqrt{x} + x}{2x - \sqrt[3]{x}}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $\ip$
  3. $1$
  4. $\frac12$
Explanation. The highest powers are $x$ in the numerator and $2x$ in the denominator: the quotient tends to $\frac12$.
am1-f-303 · level teorema · notes
Let $P$ and $Q$ be polynomials of degrees $p$ and $q$ with leading coefficients $a \neq 0$ and $b \neq 0$. Which statement about $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \frac{P(x)}{Q(x)}$ is correct?
  1. If $p = q$ the limit is $\frac ab$; if $p < q$ it is $\pm\infty$; if $p > q$ it is $0$.
  2. If $p = q$ the limit is $1$; if $p < q$ it is $0$; if $p > q$ it is $\pm\infty$ with the sign of $\frac ab$.
  3. If $p = q$ the limit is $\frac ab$; if $p < q$ it is $0$; if $p > q$ it is $\pm\infty$ with the sign of $\frac ab$.
  4. If $p = q$ the limit is $\frac ab$; if $p < q$ it is $0$; if $p > q$ it is $\ip$ in every case.
Explanation. $\frac{P(x)}{Q(x)} \sim \frac{a x^p}{b x^q} = \frac ab x^{p-q}$, which tends to $\frac ab$, to $0$ or to $\pm\infty$ according to the sign of $p - q$.
am1-f-304 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 1} \frac{x^2 - 3x + 2}{x^2 - 1}$?
  1. $-\frac12$
  2. $0$
  3. $\frac12$
  4. $\im$
Explanation. Form $\left[\frac00\right]$: $\frac{(x - 1)(x - 2)}{(x - 1)(x + 1)} = \frac{x - 2}{x + 1} \rr -\frac12$.
am1-f-563 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \left(x^3 - 100x^2 - 1000\right)$?
  1. $\im$
  2. $0$
  3. $-1000$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. A polynomial as $x \rr \pm\infty$ behaves like its highest-degree term: $x^3 \rr \ip$, whatever the coefficients of the lower-degree terms.
am1-f-564 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \im} \frac{x^4 + 1}{x^3 - 2}$?
  1. $\ip$
  2. $\im$
  3. $1$
  4. $0$
Explanation. The ratio behaves like $\frac{x^4}{x^3} = x$, which tends to $\im$ as $x \rr \im$.
am1-f-565 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \frac{3x^{5/2} - x}{x^{2} + 2x^{5/2}}$?
  1. $3$
  2. $0$
  3. $\frac32$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. In numerator and denominator the power $x^{5/2}$ dominates: the limit is that of $\frac{3x^{5/2}}{2x^{5/2}} = \frac32$.
am1-f-566 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \frac{\sqrt[3]{8x^3 + x}}{x - 1}$?
  1. $2$
  2. $8$
  3. $\frac12$
  4. $1$
Explanation. $\sqrt[3]{8x^3 + x} = 2x\sqrt[3]{1 + \frac{1}{8x^2}} \sim 2x$ and $x - 1 \sim x$: the limit is $2$.

4. Continuous functions (12)

am1-f-081 · level ipotesi · notes
Algebra of continuous functions: $f$ and $g$ are defined in a neighborhood of $x_0$. Which hypotheses are needed to conclude that $\frac{f}{g}$ is continuous at $x_0$?
  1. $f$ continuous at $x_0$ and $g(x_0) \neq 0$
  2. $f$ and $g$ continuous at $x_0$, with $g \ge 0$
  3. $f$ and $g$ continuous at $x_0$ and $g(x_0) \neq 0$
  4. $f$ and $g$ differentiable at $x_0$
Explanation. One needs continuity of both at $x_0$ and $g(x_0) \neq 0$. Differentiability is more than necessary, and without $g(x_0) \neq 0$ the quotient is not even defined at $x_0$.
am1-f-082 · level facile · notes
For which value of $k$ is the function $f(x) = x^2 + k$ for $x < 1$, $f(x) = 2x + 3$ for $x \ge 1$ continuous at $x = 1$?
  1. $k = 4$
  2. $k = 5$
  3. $k = 1$
  4. $k = 3$
Explanation. We need $\lim_{x \rr 1^-} f(x) = f(1)$: $1 + k = 5$, hence $k = 4$.
am1-f-305 · level facile · notes
On which set is the function $f(x) = \frac{\log x}{x - 2}$ (natural $\log$) continuous?
  1. $\R \setminus \{2\}$
  2. $(0, \ip)$
  3. $(0, 2) \cup (2, \ip)$
  4. $[0, 2) \cup (2, \ip)$
Explanation. Logarithm and polynomials are continuous on their domains, and a quotient of continuous functions is continuous where the denominator does not vanish: $x > 0$ and $x \neq 2$.
am1-f-306 · level teorema · notes
Continuity of composite functions theorem: which statement is correct?
  1. If $g$ is continuous at $x_0$ and $f$ is continuous at $g(x_0)$, then $f \circ g$ is continuous at $x_0$.
  2. If $f \circ g$ is continuous at $x_0$, then $g$ is continuous at $x_0$.
  3. If $g$ is continuous at $x_0$, then $f \circ g$ is continuous at $x_0$ for every $f$.
  4. If $f$ and $g$ are continuous, then $f \circ g$ is differentiable.
Explanation. If $x \rr x_0$, then $g(x) \rr g(x_0)$ and, by continuity of $f$ there, $f(g(x)) \rr f(g(x_0))$. With $f \equiv 0$ the composite is continuous even for discontinuous $g$.
am1-f-307 · level facile · notes
For which value of $a$ is the function $f(x) = \frac{\sin(3x)}{x}$ for $x \neq 0$, $f(0) = a$, continuous at $0$?
  1. $a = 0$
  2. $a = 1$
  3. $a = \frac13$
  4. $a = 3$
Explanation. $\frac{\sin 3x}{x} = 3 \cdot \frac{\sin 3x}{3x} \rr 3$, so $f(0) = 3$ is needed.
am1-f-308 · level facile · notes
The integer part function $[x]$ (the integer $n$ with $n \le x < n+1$) is continuous:
  1. on all of $\R$
  2. on $\R \setminus \Z$
  3. only at the integers
  4. at no point
Explanation. At each integer $n$ the left limit is $n - 1$ and the right limit is $n$: there is a jump. Between two consecutive integers the function is constant, hence continuous.
am1-f-309 · level facile · notes
For which $a, b$ is the function $f(x) = a x + 1$ for $x < 2$, $f(x) = b$ for $x = 2$, $f(x) = x^2 - 1$ for $x > 2$ continuous at $2$?
  1. $a = 1$, $b = 3$
  2. $a = 3$, $b = 1$
  3. $a = 1$, $b = 4$
  4. $a = 2$, $b = 3$
Explanation. The right limit is $4 - 1 = 3$, the left one $2a + 1$; we need $2a + 1 = 3$ and $b = 3$, i.e. $a = 1$, $b = 3$.
am1-f-310 · level facile · notes
Which of the following functions is NOT continuous at $x = 0$?
  1. $f(x) = |x|$
  2. $f(x) = \sqrt[3]{x}$
  3. $f(x) = \frac{x}{|x|}$ for $x \neq 0$, $f(0) = 0$
  4. $f(x) = e^{-x^2}$
Explanation. $\frac{x}{|x|}$ equals $1$ for $x > 0$ and $-1$ for $x < 0$: the one-sided limits differ (jump). The others are continuous at $0$.
am1-f-567 · level facile · notes
Let $f$ and $g$ be continuous at $x_0$. Which function may NOT be continuous at $x_0$ (or not be defined at $x_0$)?
  1. $f + g$
  2. $f \cdot g$
  3. $\frac{f}{g}$
  4. $3f - g$
Explanation. Sums, products and linear combinations of continuous functions are continuous; the quotient is so only if $g(x_0) \neq 0$.
am1-f-568 · level facile · notes
On which set is the function $f(x) = \sqrt{x - 1} + \log(3 - x)$ (natural $\log$) continuous?
  1. $[1, 3)$
  2. $(1, 3)$
  3. $[1, 3]$
  4. $(1, \ip)$
Explanation. Elementary functions are continuous on their domain: we need $x - 1 \ge 0$ and $3 - x > 0$, i.e. $1 \le x < 3$.
am1-f-569 · level facile · notes
For which value of $a$ is the function $f(x) = e^{\frac{\sin x}{x}}$ for $x \neq 0$, $f(0) = a$, continuous at $0$?
  1. $a = 1$
  2. $a = 0$
  3. $a = e^{-1}$
  4. $a = e$
Explanation. $\frac{\sin x}{x} \rr 1$ and the exponential is continuous, so $f(x) \rr e^1$ (continuity of composite functions): we need $a = e$.
am1-f-570 · level facile · notes
Is the function $f(x) = x\,[x]$, where $[x]$ is the integer part (the largest integer $\le x$), continuous at $x = 0$?
  1. no: $[x]$ has a jump at $0$
  2. yes: the left and right limits equal $0 = f(0)$
  3. no: the left limit is $-1$
  4. yes, because $[x]$ is continuous at $0$
Explanation. For $-1 \le x < 0$ we have $f(x) = -x \rr 0$, for $0 \le x < 1$ we have $f(x) = 0$: the jump of $[x]$ is cancelled by the factor $x$.

5. Comparison of infinities (12)

am1-f-083 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \frac{x^{100}}{e^{x}}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $\ip$
  3. $1$
  4. $100$
Explanation. Comparison of infinities: $\frac{x^\alpha}{b^{\lambda x}} \rr 0$ for every $\alpha > 0$, $b > 1$. The exponential beats any power.
am1-f-084 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \frac{x^2 + e^{x}}{x^3 + 2\,e^{x}}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $1$
  3. $\frac12$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. In numerator and denominator $e^x$ dominates: the quotient is asymptotic to $\frac{e^x}{2e^x} = \frac12$.
am1-f-311 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \frac{\log x}{x^{0.01}}$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $\ip$
  2. $0$
  3. $1$
  4. $100$
Explanation. The logarithm is negligible with respect to every power with positive exponent, however small: $\frac{\log x}{x^\alpha} \rr 0$ for every $\alpha > 0$.
am1-f-312 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 0^+} x \log x$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $\im$
  2. $1$
  3. $\ip$
  4. $0$
Explanation. With $t = \frac1x \rr \ip$: $x\log x = -\frac{\log t}{t} \rr 0$. The logarithm tends to $\im$ more slowly than $x$ tends to $0$.
am1-f-313 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \im} x^3 e^{x}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $\im$
  3. $\ip$
  4. $1$
Explanation. With $t = -x \rr \ip$: $x^3 e^x = -\frac{t^3}{e^t} \rr 0$, since the exponential beats every power.
am1-f-314 · level facile · notes
