Order as $x \rr \ip$, from slowest to fastest, the infinities $e^x$, $x^{10}$, $\log x$, $x^x$:
  1. $x^{10},~ \log x,~ e^x,~ x^x$
  2. $\log x,~ e^x,~ x^{10},~ x^x$
  3. $\log x,~ x^{10},~ e^x,~ x^x$
  4. $\log x,~ x^{10},~ x^x,~ e^x$
Explanation. Logarithms, powers, exponentials; and $x^x = e^{x\log x}$ beats $e^x$ because $x\log x - x \rr \ip$.
am1-f-315 · level teorema · notes
Comparison of infinities: which statement is correct as $x \rr \ip$, with $\alpha > 0$, $\beta > 0$, $b > 1$?
  1. $\frac{x^\alpha}{(\log x)^\beta} \rr 0$
  2. $\frac{(\log x)^\beta}{x^\alpha} \rr 0$ and $\frac{x^\alpha}{b^x} \rr 0$
  3. $\frac{b^x}{x^\alpha} \rr 0$
  4. $\frac{(\log x)^\beta}{x^\alpha} \rr 1$ if $\alpha = \beta$
Explanation. Every power of the logarithm is negligible with respect to every power of $x$, and every power is negligible with respect to every exponential with base $b > 1$.
am1-f-316 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \frac{e^{2x}}{e^x + x^5}$?
  1. $1$
  2. $0$
  3. $2$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. In the denominator $e^x$ dominates, so the quotient is asymptotic to $\frac{e^{2x}}{e^x} = e^x \rr \ip$.
am1-f-571 · level teorema · notes
Comparing infinities and infinitesimals as $x \rr 0^+$: for $\alpha > 0$ and $\beta > 0$, which statement is correct (natural $\log$)?
  1. $x^\alpha\,|\log x|^\beta \rr 0$
  2. $x^\alpha\,|\log x|^\beta \rr \ip$
  3. $x^\alpha\,|\log x|^\beta \rr 1$
  4. the limit of $x^\alpha\,|\log x|^\beta$ depends on how $\alpha$ compares with $\beta$
Explanation. With $x = \frac1t$, $t \rr \ip$: $x^\alpha |\log x|^\beta = \frac{(\log t)^\beta}{t^\alpha} \rr 0$ for every $\alpha, \beta > 0$ (the logarithm is negligible with respect to every power).
am1-f-572 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \frac{2^x}{3^x + x^{10}}$?
  1. $\frac23$
  2. $1$
  3. $0$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. $x^{10} = o(3^x)$, so the denominator is asymptotic to $3^x$ and the ratio to $\left(\frac23\right)^x \rr 0$.
am1-f-573 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \frac{\log(x^5)}{\log x + \sqrt[10]{x}}$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $5$
  2. $0$
  3. $1$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. $\log(x^5) = 5\log x$ and $\log x = o\left(x^{1/10}\right)$: the ratio is asymptotic to $\frac{5\log x}{x^{1/10}} \rr 0$. The value $5$ would come from wrongly neglecting $\sqrt[10]{x}$.
am1-f-574 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} x^{1/x}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $e$
  3. $\ip$
  4. $1$
Explanation. $x^{1/x} = e^{\frac{\log x}{x}}$ and $\frac{\log x}{x} \rr 0$, so the limit is $e^0 = 1$.

6. Fundamental limits and asymptotic estimates (12)

am1-f-085 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{1 - \cos x}{x^2}$?
  1. $1$
  2. $\frac12$
  3. $0$
  4. $2$
Explanation. This is a standard limit: $1 - \cos x \sim \frac{x^2}{2}$ as $x \rr 0$.
am1-f-086 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{\sqrt[3]{1 + x} - 1}{x}$?
  1. $\frac13$
  2. $3$
  3. $1$
  4. $0$
Explanation. Standard limit of the power, with $\alpha = \frac13$: $\frac{(1+x)^\alpha - 1}{x} \rr \alpha$.
am1-f-087 · level facile · notes
The function $f(x) = \frac{\sin x}{x}$, defined for $x \neq 0$:
  1. cannot be extended continuously at $0$
  2. is extended continuously by setting $f(0) = 0$
  3. has a vertical asymptote at $x = 0$
  4. is extended continuously by setting $f(0) = 1$
Explanation. Since $\frac{\sin x}{x} \rr 1$ as $x \rr 0$, setting $f(0) = 1$ gives a function continuous on $\R$.
am1-f-317 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{e^{5x} - 1}{x}$?
  1. $1$
  2. $\frac15$
  3. $5$
  4. $0$
Explanation. Standard limit $\frac{e^t - 1}{t} \rr 1$ with $t = 5x$: $\frac{e^{5x} - 1}{x} = 5 \cdot \frac{e^{5x} - 1}{5x} \rr 5$.
am1-f-318 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{\tan x}{x}$?
  1. $1$
  2. $0$
  3. $\ip$
  4. it does not exist
Explanation. $\frac{\tan x}{x} = \frac{\sin x}{x} \cdot \frac{1}{\cos x} \rr 1 \cdot 1 = 1$.
am1-f-319 · level facile · notes
Using asymptotic estimates as $x \rr 0$, what is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{\log(1 + 2x)}{\sin(3x)}$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $\frac32$
  2. $1$
  3. $6$
  4. $\frac23$
Explanation. $\log(1 + 2x) \sim 2x$ and $\sin 3x \sim 3x$, so the quotient is asymptotic to $\frac{2x}{3x} = \frac23$.
am1-f-320 · level teorema · notes
Which standard limit as $x \rr 0$ is correct (natural $\log$, $a > 0$, $\alpha \in \R$)?
  1. $\frac{a^x - 1}{x} \rr a$
  2. $\frac{a^x - 1}{x} \rr \log a$
  3. $\frac{(1 + x)^\alpha - 1}{x} \rr 1$
  4. $\frac{\log(1 + x)}{x} \rr 0$
Explanation. $a^x - 1 = e^{x\log a} - 1 \sim x\log a$. Instead $\frac{(1+x)^\alpha - 1}{x} \rr \alpha$ and $\frac{\log(1+x)}{x} \rr 1$.
am1-f-321 · level facile · notes
As $x \rr 0$, what is the function $1 - \cos(2x)$ asymptotic to?
  1. $2x$
  2. $x^2$
  3. $2x^2$
  4. $\frac{x^2}{2}$
Explanation. $1 - \cos t \sim \frac{t^2}{2}$ with $t = 2x$: $\frac{(2x)^2}{2} = 2x^2$.
am1-f-575 · level facile · notes
As $x \rr 0$ we have $\sin x \sim x$. What does this estimate say about the graph of $y = \sin x$ near the origin?
  1. it is tangent at the origin to the line $y = x$
  2. it coincides with the line $y = x$ in a neighborhood of $0$
  3. it lies above the line $y = x$ for every $x > 0$
  4. it is tangent at the origin to the $x$-axis
Explanation. $\frac{\sin x}{x} \rr 1$ means that the graph approaches the bisector and has slope $1$ at $0$; but $\sin x < x$ for $x > 0$, so it does not coincide with it and lies below.
am1-f-576 · level facile · notes
As $x \rr 0$ we have $1 - \cos x \sim \frac{x^2}{2}$. Near $x = 0$, the graph of $y = \cos x$ is well approximated by:
  1. the line $y = 1 - x$
  2. the parabola $y = \frac{x^2}{2}$
  3. the parabola $y = 1 - \frac{x^2}{2}$
  4. the parabola $y = 1 + \frac{x^2}{2}$
Explanation. From the estimate, $\cos x = 1 - \frac{x^2}{2} + o(x^2)$: near $0$ the graph of the cosine is close to that of the downward parabola with vertex $(0, 1)$.
am1-f-577 · level teorema · notes
Standard limit of the power: for fixed $\alpha \in \R$, which estimate holds as $x \rr 0$?
  1. $(1 + x)^\alpha - 1 \sim x^\alpha$
  2. $(1 + x)^\alpha - 1 \sim \alpha\,x$
  3. $(1 + x)^\alpha - 1 \sim \frac{x}{\alpha}$
  4. $(1 + x)^\alpha - 1 \sim \alpha\,x^{\alpha - 1}$
Explanation. $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{(1 + x)^\alpha - 1}{x} = \alpha$, i.e. the derivative of $t^\alpha$ at $t = 1$. For example $\sqrt{1 + x} - 1 \sim \frac{x}{2}$.
am1-f-578 · level facile · notes
Using the asymptotic estimates as $x \rr 0$ ($e^t - 1 \sim t$, $\sin t \sim t$), what is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{e^{x^2} - 1}{x \sin(2x)}$?
  1. $2$
  2. $1$
  3. $0$
  4. $\frac12$
Explanation. $e^{x^2} - 1 \sim x^2$ and $x \sin(2x) \sim 2x^2$: the limit is $\frac{x^2}{2x^2} = \frac12$.

7. Asymptotic expansions (12)

am1-f-088 · level facile · notes
Which statement is correct as $x \rr 0$?
  1. $x^2 = o(x^3)$
  2. $x^3 = o(x^2)$
  3. $x = o(x^2)$
  4. $\sin x = o(x)$
Explanation. $\frac{x^3}{x^2} = x \rr 0$. As $x \rr 0$ higher powers are negligible with respect to lower ones; $\frac{\sin x}{x} \rr 1 \neq 0$.
am1-f-089 · level facile · notes
Which asymptotic expansion as $x \rr 0$ is correct?
  1. $\log(1 + x) = x + o(x)$
  2. $e^x = x + o(x)$
  3. $\sin x = 1 + o(x)$
  4. $\log(1 + x) = 1 + x + o(x)$
Explanation. From the standard limit $\frac{\log(1+x)}{x} \rr 1$ we get $\log(1+x) = x + o(x)$. Instead $e^x = 1 + x + o(x)$ and $\sin x = x + o(x)$.
am1-f-322 · level facile · notes
As $x \rr 0$, what is $o(x^2) + o(x^3)$?
  1. $o(x^3)$
  2. $o(x^2)$
  3. $o(x^5)$
  4. $0$
Explanation. A function that is $o(x^3)$ is also $o(x^2)$ as $x \rr 0$; the sum is therefore negligible with respect to $x^2$, but in general not with respect to $x^3$.
am1-f-323 · level facile · notes
As $x \rr 0$, what is $x \cdot o(x^2)$?
  1. $o(x^2)$
  2. $o(x)$
  3. $x^3$
  4. $o(x^3)$
Explanation. If $\frac{g(x)}{x^2} \rr 0$ then $\frac{x\,g(x)}{x^3} \rr 0$: multiplying by $x$ raises the order by one.
am1-f-324 · level facile · notes
From the expansion $e^t = 1 + t + o(t)$ as $t \rr 0$, which expansion of $e^{-3x}$ as $x \rr 0$ is correct?
  1. $1 - 3x + o(x)$
  2. $1 + 3x + o(x)$
  3. $-3x + o(x)$
  4. $1 - 3x^2 + o(x^2)$
Explanation. Substitute $t = -3x$: $e^{-3x} = 1 - 3x + o(-3x) = 1 - 3x + o(x)$.
am1-f-325 · level facile · notes
As $x \rr \ip$, which statement is correct?
  1. $x^2 = o(x)$
  2. $e^x = o(x^{100})$
  3. $x = o(x^2)$
  4. $\log x = o(1)$
Explanation. $\frac{x}{x^2} = \frac1x \rr 0$. As $x \rr \ip$ (unlike $x \rr 0$) it is the lower powers that are negligible.
am1-f-326 · level teorema · notes
As $x \rr x_0$ and for functions $f$, $g$ with $g \neq 0$ near $x_0$, which relation between the symbols $\sim$ and $o$ is correct?
  1. $f \sim g$ if and only if $f - g \rr 0$
  2. $f \sim g$ if and only if $f = g + o(g)$
  3. $f = o(g)$ if and only if $f \sim g$
  4. $f \sim g$ implies $f = o(g)$
Explanation. $\frac fg \rr 1$ is equivalent to $\frac{f - g}{g} \rr 0$, i.e. $f - g = o(g)$. The difference need not tend to $0$ (e.g. $x^2 + x \sim x^2$ as $x \rr \ip$).
am1-f-327 · level facile · notes
Using $\sin x = x + o(x)$ and $e^x = 1 + x + o(x)$ as $x \rr 0$, what is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{e^x - 1 + \sin x}{x}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $1$
  3. $\ip$
  4. $2$
Explanation. The numerator is $x + o(x) + x + o(x) = 2x + o(x)$, so the quotient tends to $2$.
am1-f-579 · level facile · notes
As $x \rr 0$, which equality between “little $o$” symbols is correct?
  1. $o(x^2) \cdot o(x^3) = o(x^5)$
  2. $o(x^2) - o(x^2) = 0$
  3. $\frac{o(x^2)}{x} = o(x^2)$
  4. $o(x^2) + o(x^3) = o(x^3)$
Explanation. The product of a function negligible with respect to $x^2$ and one negligible with respect to $x^3$ is negligible with respect to $x^5$. Two different $o(x^2)$ do not cancel, and $\frac{o(x^2)}{x} = o(x)$.
am1-f-580 · level facile · notes
Using $\sin t = t + o(t)$ and $\log(1 + t) = t + o(t)$ as $t \rr 0$, which expansion of $\log(1 + \sin x)$ as $x \rr 0$ is correct?
  1. $1 + x + o(x)$
  2. $x + o(x)$
  3. $x^2 + o(x^2)$
  4. $\sin 1 + o(x)$
Explanation. With $t = \sin x \rr 0$: $\log(1 + \sin x) = \sin x + o(\sin x) = x + o(x)$, because $o(\sin x) = o(x)$.
am1-f-581 · level facile · notes
As $x \rr 0$, which function is $o(x)$, i.e. such that $\frac{f(x)}{x} \rr 0$?
  1. $2x$
  2. $\sin x$
  3. $x^2 + x^3$
  4. $\sqrt{|x|}$
Explanation. $\frac{x^2 + x^3}{x} = x + x^2 \rr 0$. Instead $\frac{2x}{x} = 2$, $\frac{\sin x}{x} \rr 1$ and $\frac{\sqrt{|x|}}{|x|} \rr \ip$.
am1-f-582 · level facile · notes
Knowing that, as $x \rr 0$, $\cos x = 1 - \frac{x^2}{2} + o(x^2)$ and $\log(1 + x) = x + o(x)$, what is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{\cos x - 1}{x \log(1 + x)}$?
  1. $\frac12$
  2. $0$
  3. $-1$
  4. $-\frac12$
Explanation. The numerator is $-\frac{x^2}{2} + o(x^2)$, the denominator $x^2 + o(x^2)$: the limit is $-\frac12$.

8. Intermediate zero theorem and bisection method (12)

am1-f-090 · level teorema · notes
Which statement is the intermediate zero theorem (Bolzano's theorem)?
  1. If $f$ is continuous on $[a,b]$ and $f(a)\,f(b) < 0$, then there exists $c \in (a,b)$ with $f(c) = 0$.
  2. If $f$ is continuous on $[a,b]$ and $f(a)\,f(b) < 0$, then there exists a unique $c \in (a,b)$ with $f(c) = 0$.
  3. If $f(a)\,f(b) < 0$, then there exists $c \in (a,b)$ with $f(c) = 0$.
  4. If $f$ is continuous on $[a,b]$ and $f(a)\,f(b) > 0$, then $f$ has no zeros in $[a,b]$.
Explanation. Uniqueness additionally requires strict monotonicity; without continuity the zero may be missing (a jump); with $f(a)f(b) > 0$ zeros may still exist ($x^2 - \frac14$ on $[-1,1]$).
am1-f-091 · level facile · notes
Bisection method on $[0, 4]$: after $n$ iterations the interval containing the zero has length $\frac{b - a}{2^n}$. What is the smallest $n$ giving length $\le \frac{1}{16}$?
  1. $6$
  2. $4$
  3. $5$
  4. $64$
Explanation. $\frac{4}{2^n} \le \frac{1}{16} \iff 2^n \ge 64 \iff n \ge 6$. $4$ forgets the initial length $b - a = 4$.
am1-f-092 · level ipotesi · notes
Intermediate zero theorem for $f$ continuous on $[a,b]$ with $f(a)\,f(b) < 0$: it is proved with the sequences $a_n$, $b_n$ of the endpoints of the bisection intervals. Which results are needed?
  1. the Weierstrass theorem, Fermat's theorem and the continuity of $f$
  2. Lagrange's theorem, the differentiability of $f$ and the comparison theorem
  3. the monotone sequence theorem, the continuity of $f$ and sign preservation
  4. the ratio test for $b_n - a_n$ and Taylor's formula for $f$
Explanation. $a_n$ increases and $b_n$ decreases, both bounded: they converge to the same $\ell$ (since $b_n - a_n = \frac{b-a}{2^n}$). By continuity and sign preservation $f(\ell)^2 \le 0$, hence $f(\ell) = 0$.
am1-f-328 · level facile · notes
By the intermediate zero theorem, in which interval does the equation $x^3 + x - 3 = 0$ certainly have a solution?
  1. $[0, 1]$
  2. $[1, 2]$
  3. $[2, 3]$
  4. $[-1, 0]$
Explanation. $f(x) = x^3 + x - 3$ is continuous with $f(1) = -1 < 0$ and $f(2) = 7 > 0$. On $[0,1]$ and $[-1,0]$ it is negative at both endpoints, on $[2,3]$ positive.
am1-f-329 · level facile · notes
Bisection method for $f(x) = x^2 - 2$ on $[1, 2]$ (keep the half where $f$ changes sign). What is the interval after two steps?
  1. $[1, 1.5]$
  2. $[1.5, 2]$
  3. $[1.5, 1.75]$
  4. $[1.25, 1.5]$
Explanation. $f(1.5) = 0.25 > 0$: keep $[1, 1.5]$. $f(1.25) = -0.4375 < 0$: keep $[1.25, 1.5]$, which contains $\sqrt2 \approx 1.414$.
am1-f-330 · level facile · notes
Let $f$ be continuous on $[a, b]$, strictly increasing, with $f(a) < 0 < f(b)$. How many zeros does $f$ have in $[a, b]$?
  1. exactly one
  2. at least two
  3. none
  4. infinitely many
Explanation. The intermediate zero theorem gives at least one zero; strict monotonicity rules out a second one (it would give $f(x_1) = f(x_2)$ with $x_1 \neq x_2$).
am1-f-331 · level facile · notes
Bisection on $[a, b] = [0, 1]$: the approximation of the zero is the midpoint of the interval after $n$ steps, with error at most $\frac{b - a}{2^{n+1}}$. How many steps suffice for an error $\le 10^{-3}$?
  1. $3$
  2. $10$
  3. $9$
  4. $1000$
Explanation. We need $\frac{1}{2^{n+1}} \le \frac{1}{1000}$, i.e. $2^{n+1} \ge 1000$: $2^{10} = 1024$, so $n + 1 = 10$ and $n = 9$.
am1-f-332 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $f(x) = \frac1x$ for $x \neq 0$ and $f(0) = 1$, on $[-1, 1]$. We have $f(-1)\,f(1) < 0$, but $f$ has no zeros. Which hypothesis of the intermediate zero theorem is missing?
  1. the differentiability of $f$
  2. the continuity of $f$ on $[-1, 1]$
  3. the monotonicity of $f$
  4. that the interval be open
Explanation. $f$ has a vertical asymptote at $0$: it is not continuous, and without continuity the sign can change without passing through zero.
am1-f-583 · level facile · notes
Bisection method for $f(x) = x^3 - 5$ on $[1, 2]$, where $f(1) < 0 < f(2)$ (at each step one keeps the half at whose endpoints $f$ has opposite signs). What is the interval after the first step?
  1. $\left[\frac32, 2\right]$
  2. $\left[1, \frac32\right]$
  3. $\left[\frac54, \frac32\right]$
  4. $\left[\frac74, 2\right]$
Explanation. At the midpoint $f\left(\frac32\right) = \frac{27}{8} - 5 < 0$: the sign change is between $\frac32$ and $2$.
am1-f-584 · level facile · notes
How many real solutions does the equation $e^x = 3 - x$ have?
  1. exactly one, in $(1, 2)$
  2. exactly one, in $(0, 1)$
  3. two
  4. none
Explanation. $g(x) = e^x + x - 3$ is continuous and strictly increasing, with $g(0) = -2 < 0$ and $g(1) = e - 2 > 0$: by the zeros theorem there is a zero in $(0, 1)$, and it is unique.
am1-f-585 · level facile · notes
For which function continuous on $[0, 1]$ does the zeros theorem (which requires $f(0)\,f(1) < 0$) guarantee a zero in $(0, 1)$?
  1. $f(x) = x^2 + \frac14$
  2. $f(x) = \left(x - \frac12\right)^2$
  3. $f(x) = x^2 - \frac14$
  4. $f(x) = 1 - x^2$
Explanation. $f(0) = -\frac14 < 0 < \frac34 = f(1)$. The second has a zero at $\frac12$, but with $f(0)\,f(1) > 0$ the theorem does not guarantee it; the fourth has $f(1) = 0$, the first has no zeros.
am1-f-586 · level facile · notes
In the bisection method for a continuous function $f$ on $[a, b]$ with $f(a)\,f(b) < 0$, which half of the interval is kept at each step (if the midpoint is not a zero)?
  1. the half where $|f|$ is smaller at the midpoint
  2. always the left half
  3. the half where $f$ is increasing
  4. the half at whose endpoints $f$ takes values of opposite sign
Explanation. In this way every interval still satisfies the hypothesis of the zeros theorem and contains a zero, while its width is halved.

9. Weierstrass theorem and intermediate value theorem (12)

am1-f-093 · level teorema · notes
Which statement is the Weierstrass theorem?
  1. If $f$ is continuous on $(a,b)$, then it has a maximum and a minimum in $(a,b)$.
  2. If $f: [a,b] \rr \R$ is continuous on $[a,b]$, then it has a maximum $M$ and a minimum $m$ in $[a,b]$.
  3. If $f: [a,b] \rr \R$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$, then it has a maximum and a minimum in $[a,b]$.
  4. If $f: [a,b] \rr \R$ is continuous, the maximum and the minimum are attained at the endpoints $a$ and $b$.
Explanation. Continuity and a closed bounded interval are needed. $f(x) = x$ on $(0,1)$ has no maximum; $\sin x$ on $[0, \pi]$ has its maximum at $\frac\pi2$, not at an endpoint.
am1-f-094 · level ipotesi · notes
$f(x) = x$ on $(0, 1)$ is continuous but has neither a maximum nor a minimum. Which hypothesis of the Weierstrass theorem is missing?
  1. a closed interval
  2. continuity of $f$
  3. differentiability of $f$
  4. monotonicity of $f$
Explanation. The theorem requires $f$ continuous on a closed bounded interval $[a,b]$. Here $f$ is continuous, differentiable and monotone, but the interval is open.
am1-f-095 · level facile · notes
By the corollary of the intermediate value theorem, what is the image $f([-1, 2])$ of $f(x) = x^2$?
  1. $[1, 4]$
  2. $[-1, 4]$
  3. $[0, 4]$
  4. $[0, 2]$
Explanation. $f([a,b]) = [m, M]$ with $m = \min f = f(0) = 0$ and $M = \max f = f(2) = 4$. $[1,4]$ only uses the values at the endpoints.
am1-f-333 · level facile · notes
What are the absolute maximum and minimum of $f(x) = x^2 - 2x$ on $[0, 3]$?
  1. maximum $0$, minimum $-1$
  2. maximum $3$, minimum $0$
  3. maximum $9$, minimum $-1$
  4. maximum $3$, minimum $-1$
Explanation. $f(x) = (x - 1)^2 - 1$: the minimum is $f(1) = -1$, the maximum is found at the endpoints, $f(0) = 0$ and $f(3) = 3$.
am1-f-334 · level teorema · notes
Intermediate value theorem: let $f: [a, b] \rr \R$ be continuous. Which statement is correct?
  1. $f$ takes every value between its minimum $m$ and its maximum $M$.
  2. $f$ takes every value between $f(a)$ and $f(b)$, and no other.
  3. $f$ takes every real value.
  4. $f$ takes each value at most once.
Explanation. By Weierstrass $m$ and $M$ exist; applying the zero theorem to $f - y$ between the minimum and maximum points, every $y \in [m, M]$ is attained. $f$ may also take values outside $[f(a), f(b)]$.
am1-f-335 · level facile · notes
Existence theorem for the $n$-th root: for an integer $n \ge 1$ and $y \ge 0$ there is a unique $x \ge 0$ with $x^n = y$. Which tool proves it?
  1. Newton's formula applied to $(1 + x)^n$
  2. Fermat's theorem applied to $x^n - y$
  3. the intermediate value theorem applied to $x^n$ on $[0, b]$, with $b^n \ge y$
  4. the ratio test for the sequence $y^n$
Explanation. $x^n$ is continuous, equals $0$ at $0$ and $b^n \ge y$ at $b$ (e.g. $b = 1 + y$): it takes the value $y$; uniqueness follows from strict monotonicity.
am1-f-336 · level facile · notes
The function $f(x) = \frac{1}{x}$ on $[1, \ip)$ is continuous and bounded. What can be said about maximum and minimum?
  1. it has maximum $1$ and minimum $0$
  2. it has maximum $1$ but no minimum ($\inf f = 0$)
  3. it has minimum $0$ but no maximum ($\sup f = 1$)
  4. it has neither maximum nor minimum ($\sup f = 1$, $\inf f = 0$)
Explanation. $f(1) = 1$ is the maximum; $f(x) \rr 0$ as $x \rr \ip$ but never equals $0$. Weierstrass does not apply because the interval is unbounded.
am1-f-337 · level facile · notes
By the intermediate value theorem, what is the image of $[0, \pi]$ under $f(x) = \sin x$?
  1. $[-1, 1]$
  2. $[0, \pi]$
  3. $\{0\}$
  4. $[0, 1]$
Explanation. $f$ is continuous, with minimum $0$ (at $0$ and $\pi$) and maximum $1$ (at $\frac\pi2$): the image is $[0, 1]$. Looking only at the endpoints ($\sin 0 = \sin\pi = 0$) would give $\{0\}$.
am1-f-587 · level facile · notes
By the existence theorem for the $n$-th root, the equation $x^5 = 7$ in the unknown $x \ge 0$:
  1. has a unique solution, $x = \sqrt[5]{7}$
  2. has two solutions, $x = \pm\sqrt[5]{7}$
  3. has no solutions, because $\sqrt[5]{7}$ is not rational
  4. has a solution only if $7$ is the fifth power of an integer
Explanation. For every $y \ge 0$ and every integer $n \ge 1$ there is a unique $x \ge 0$ with $x^n = y$ (continuity and strict monotonicity of $x^n$). The solution is irrational but real.
am1-f-588 · level facile · notes
Let $n$ be an odd integer and $y < 0$. How many real solutions does the equation $x^n = y$ have?
  1. none, because $y < 0$
  2. two, opposite to each other
  3. exactly one, $x = -\sqrt[n]{-y}$
  4. $n$ distinct solutions
Explanation. For odd $n$, $(-x)^n = -x^n$: from $(-x)^n = y$ we get $x^n = -y > 0$, which has the unique positive solution $\sqrt[n]{-y}$. $x^n$ is strictly increasing on $\R$, so the solution is unique.
am1-f-589 · level facile · notes
Applying the intermediate value theorem to $f(x) = x^3$ on the interval $[1, 2]$, which value is certainly taken by $f$ on $[1, 2]$?
  1. $9$
  2. $5$
  3. $0$
  4. $-1$
Explanation. $f$ is continuous with minimum $f(1) = 1$ and maximum $f(2) = 8$: it takes every value in $[1, 8]$, hence also $5$. The other values lie outside $[1, 8]$.
am1-f-590 · level facile · notes
What are the absolute maximum and minimum of $f(x) = |x - 1|$ on the interval $[-1, 2]$?
  1. maximum $1$, minimum $0$
  2. maximum $2$, minimum $1$
  3. maximum $3$, minimum $0$
  4. maximum $2$, minimum $0$
Explanation. They exist by Weierstrass ($f$ continuous on a closed bounded interval). $f(-1) = 2$, $f(2) = 1$ and the minimum is $f(1) = 0$, at the corner point (where $f$ is not differentiable).

10. Monotone functions on an interval and invertibility (12)

am1-f-096 · level teorema · notes
Let $f: I \rr \R$ be continuous on an interval $I$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $f$ is invertible if and only if it is monotone (not necessarily strictly); the inverse is continuous.
  2. $f$ is invertible if and only if it is strictly monotone; the inverse may fail to be continuous.
  3. $f$ is invertible if and only if it is differentiable with $f' \neq 0$; the inverse is differentiable.
  4. $f$ is invertible if and only if it is strictly monotone; the inverse is continuous.
Explanation. This is the invertibility theorem for continuous functions on an interval, and the inverse is continuous too. A function constant on a subinterval is monotone but not injective; $x^3$ is invertible but $f'(0) = 0$.
am1-f-097 · level facile · notes
Let $f: (a,b) \rr \R$ be monotone. For every $c \in (a,b)$:
  1. the right and left limits as $x \rr c$ exist and are finite
  2. $f$ is continuous at $c$
  3. the right and left limits at $c$ exist but may be infinite
  4. $f$ is differentiable at $c$
Explanation. Monotonicity theorem for functions: at interior points the one-sided limits exist and are finite (they are a supremum and an infimum). They may differ, though: $f$ can have jumps.
am1-f-338 · level facile · notes
Let $f$ be increasing on $\R$. Which statement about the limit of $f$ as $x \rr \ip$ is correct?
  1. the limit is always $\ip$
  2. the limit may not exist (oscillation)
  3. the limit exists and equals $\sup_{\R} f$ (finite or $\ip$)
  4. the limit exists and is always finite
Explanation. For a monotone function the limit at the endpoint exists and is the supremum of the values: $\arctan x \rr \frac\pi2$ (finite), $x \rr \ip$.
am1-f-339 · level facile · notes
The function $f(x) = x^3 + x$ is continuous and strictly increasing on $\R$. What can be said about its inverse?
  1. it exists on $\R$ and is continuous and strictly increasing
  2. it exists only on $[0, \ip)$
  3. it exists but is discontinuous at $0$
  4. it does not exist because $f$ is not a power
Explanation. $f$ is injective and, since $f \rr \pm\infty$ as $x \rr \pm\infty$, onto $\R$; by the theorem on continuous monotone functions the inverse is continuous and increasing.
am1-f-340 · level facile · notes
Can a monotone function $f: (a, b) \rr \R$ be discontinuous at a point $c \in (a, b)$?
  1. no, a monotone function is always continuous
  2. yes, with a vertical asymptote at $c$
  3. yes, with a removable discontinuity
  4. yes, but only with a jump
Explanation. The right and left limits at $c$ exist, are finite, and enclose $f(c)$; if one of them differs from $f(c)$ there is a jump. The integer part is an example.
am1-f-341 · level ipotesi · notes
To conclude that a continuous injective function $f$ is strictly monotone, which hypothesis on the domain is needed?
  1. that it be bounded
  2. that it be an interval
  3. that it be closed
  4. that it contain $0$
Explanation. On an interval continuity and injectivity give strict monotonicity. On $\R \setminus \{0\}$, $f(x) = \frac1x$ is continuous and injective but not monotone.
am1-f-342 · level facile · notes
Let $f: [0, 2] \rr \R$ be continuous and strictly decreasing, with $f(0) = 5$ and $f(2) = 1$. What is the domain of the inverse $f^{-1}$?
  1. $[0, 2]$
  2. $[0, 5]$
  3. $[1, 5]$
  4. $[5, 1]$
Explanation. The image of $f$ is the interval between $f(2) = 1$ and $f(0) = 5$, i.e. $[1, 5]$, which is the domain of $f^{-1}$.
am1-f-343 · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = [x] + x$, where $[x]$ is the integer part (the largest integer $\le x$). What are the left and right limits at $x = 1$?
  1. $2$ and $2$
  2. $1$ and $2$
  3. $0$ and $1$
  4. $1$ and $1$
Explanation. $f$ is increasing. As $x \rr 1^-$: $[x] = 0$, $f \rr 1$; as $x \rr 1^+$: $[x] = 1$, $f \rr 2$. A jump of size $1$.
am1-f-591 · level facile · notes
Let $f$ be (strictly) increasing on $(0, 1)$ with $\lim_{x \rr 1^-} f(x) = 5$. What can be said about the values of $f$?
  1. $f(x) < 5$ for every $x \in (0, 1)$
  2. $f(x) \le 4$ for every $x \in (0, 1)$
  3. $f(x) = 5$ for some $x \in (0, 1)$
  4. $f(x) > 5$ for $x$ close to $1$
Explanation. For an increasing function the limit at $1^-$ is $\sup_{(0,1)} f = 5$; if $f(x_1) \ge 5$, then for $x_2 > x_1$ we would have $f(x_2) > 5$, contradicting $\sup f = 5$.
am1-f-592 · level facile · notes
Which function continuous on $[0, 2]$ is NOT invertible on $[0, 2]$?
  1. $f(x) = x^3$
  2. $f(x) = e^{-x}$
  3. $f(x) = x^2 - 2x$
  4. $f(x) = \sqrt{x}$
Explanation. A continuous function on an interval is invertible if and only if it is strictly monotone. $x^2 - 2x$ decreases on $[0, 1]$ and increases on $[1, 2]$ (for instance $f(0) = f(2) = 0$).
am1-f-593 · level facile · notes
Let $f: [0, 3] \rr \R$ be continuous and injective, with $f(0) = 4$ and $f(3) = -2$. What is concluded?
  1. $f$ is strictly increasing on $[0, 3]$
  2. $f$ is strictly decreasing and $f([0, 3]) = [-2, 4]$
  3. $f$ may have a minimum at an interior point
  4. $f([0, 3]) = [-2, 4]$, but $f$ may fail to be monotone
Explanation. On an interval a continuous injective function is strictly monotone; since $f(0) > f(3)$ it is decreasing, and by the intermediate values the image is $[f(3), f(0)]$.
am1-f-594 · level facile · notes
Can a monotone function $f: \R \rr \R$ have infinitely many points of discontinuity?
  1. no, at most one
  2. no, at most finitely many
  3. yes: the integer part $[x]$ (non-decreasing) jumps at every integer
  4. yes, but only if it is unbounded
Explanation. $[x]$, the largest integer $\le x$, is monotone and has a jump at every $n \in \Z$. The discontinuities of a monotone function are only jumps, but there may be infinitely many.

Part: Derivatives (120)

1. The derivative function (12)

am1-f-098 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about continuity and differentiability at a point $x_0 \in (a,b)$ is correct?
  1. If $f$ is differentiable at $x_0$, then it is continuous at $x_0$.
  2. If $f$ is continuous at $x_0$, then it is differentiable at $x_0$.
  3. $f$ is continuous at $x_0$ if and only if it is differentiable at $x_0$.
  4. If $f$ is differentiable at $x_0$, then $f'$ is continuous at $x_0$.
Explanation. $f(x_0 + h) - f(x_0) = \frac{f(x_0+h) - f(x_0)}{h}\cdot h \rr f'(x_0)\cdot 0 = 0$. The converse is false: $|x|$ is continuous but not differentiable at $0$.
am1-f-099 · level facile · notes
By definition, the derivative of $f$ at $x_0$ is the limit (if it exists and is finite):
  1. $\lim_{h \rr 0} \big(f(x_0 + h) - f(x_0)\big)$
  2. $\lim_{h \rr 0} \frac{h}{f(x_0 + h) - f(x_0)}$
  3. $\lim_{h \rr 0} \frac{f(x_0 + h) - f(x_0)}{h}$
  4. $\lim_{h \rr 0} \frac{f(x_0 + h) - f(x_0 - h)}{h}$
Explanation. It is the limit of the difference quotient, the slope of the secants. The first limit is $0$ for every continuous function; the last equals $2f'(x_0)$ if $f$ is differentiable.
am1-f-100 · level facile · notes
The function $f(x) = |x|$ at the point $x_0 = 0$ is:
  1. differentiable with $f'(0) = 0$
  2. continuous but not differentiable
  3. not continuous
  4. differentiable with $f'(0) = 1$
Explanation. $\lim_{x\rr0}|x| = 0 = f(0)$, but the difference quotient $\frac{|h|}{h}$ is $1$ for $h > 0$ and $-1$ for $h < 0$: the limit does not exist.
am1-f-344 · level facile · notes
What is the tangent line to the graph of $f(x) = x^2$ at the point with abscissa $x_0 = 3$?
  1. $y = 6x$
  2. $y = 3x$
  3. $y = 6x - 9$
  4. $y = 9x - 18$
Explanation. $f(3) = 9$ and $f'(3) = 6$: $y = f(x_0) + f'(x_0)(x - x_0) = 9 + 6(x - 3) = 6x - 9$.
am1-f-345 · level facile · notes
Using the definition (limit of the difference quotient), what is the derivative of $f(x) = \frac{1}{x}$ at $x_0 = 2$?
  1. $-\frac14$
  2. $\frac12$
  3. $-\frac12$
  4. $\frac14$
Explanation. $\frac{\frac{1}{2 + h} - \frac12}{h} = \frac{-h}{2h(2 + h)} = -\frac{1}{2(2 + h)} \rr -\frac14$.
am1-f-346 · level facile · notes
What is the slope of the secant line to the graph of $f(x) = x^3$ through the points with abscissas $1$ and $3$?
  1. $26$
  2. $27$
  3. $3$
  4. $13$
Explanation. Slope $= \frac{f(3) - f(1)}{3 - 1} = \frac{27 - 1}{2} = 13$ (difference quotient with $h = 2$).
am1-f-347 · level facile · notes
Using the tangent line at $x_0 = 4$ to $f(x) = \sqrt x$, what is the approximation of $\sqrt{4.1}$?
  1. $2.05$
  2. $2.025$
  3. $2.1$
  4. $2.01$
Explanation. $f(4) = 2$, $f'(4) = \frac{1}{2\sqrt4} = \frac14$: $\sqrt{4.1} \approx 2 + \frac14 \cdot 0.1 = 2.025$ (the true value is $2.0248\ldots$).
am1-f-348 · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = x^2$ for $x \le 1$ and $f(x) = 2x - 1$ for $x > 1$. At $x_0 = 1$ the function is:
  1. continuous but not differentiable
  2. discontinuous
  3. differentiable with $f'(1) = 2$
  4. differentiable with $f'(1) = 1$
Explanation. Both expressions equal $1$ at $x = 1$ (continuity) and have derivative $2$ at $x = 1$: left and right derivatives coincide, $f'(1) = 2$.
am1-f-595 · level facile · notes
What is the equation of the line through the points $(1, 2)$ and $(3, 8)$?
  1. $y = 3x + 2$
  2. $y = 3x - 1$
  3. $y = 2x$
  4. $y = \frac13 x + \frac53$
Explanation. The slope is $m = \frac{8 - 2}{3 - 1} = 3$ and the line is $y = 2 + 3(x - 1) = 3x - 1$. The fourth uses the inverted ratio $\frac{\Delta x}{\Delta y}$.
am1-f-596 · level teorema · notes
Let $f$ be differentiable at $x_0$. Which statement about the linear approximation of $f$ near $x_0$ is correct (as $h \rr 0$)?
  1. $f(x_0 + h) = f(x_0) + f'(x_0)\,h$ for every small enough $h$
  2. $f(x_0 + h) = f(x_0) + f'(x_0)\,h + o(h^2)$
  3. $f(x_0 + h) - f(x_0) = o(h)$
  4. $f(x_0 + h) = f(x_0) + f'(x_0)\,h + o(h)$
Explanation. From the definition, $\frac{f(x_0 + h) - f(x_0)}{h} - f'(x_0) \rr 0$, i.e. the error of the tangent line is $o(h)$. An error $o(h^2)$ is not guaranteed; $f(x_0 + h) - f(x_0) = o(h)$ holds only if $f'(x_0) = 0$.
am1-f-597 · level facile · notes
At which point is the tangent line to the graph of $f(x) = x^2 - 4x$ horizontal?
  1. $x = 2$
  2. $x = 0$
  3. $x = 4$
  4. $x = -2$
Explanation. The tangent is horizontal where $f'(x) = 2x - 4 = 0$, i.e. at $x = 2$ (the vertex of the parabola). At $0$ and $4$ it is $f$, not $f'$, that vanishes.
am1-f-598 · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = x\,|x|$. Using the definition of derivative, what is $f'(0)$?
  1. $1$
  2. $-1$
  3. $0$
  4. it does not exist, because $|x|$ is not differentiable at $0$
Explanation. $\frac{f(h) - f(0)}{h} = \frac{h|h|}{h} = |h| \rr 0$. The product with $x$ “absorbs” the corner of $|x|$.

2. Derivatives of elementary functions (12)

am1-f-101 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of $f(x) = \log x$ (base-$e$ logarithm) for $x > 0$?
  1. $\frac{1}{x}$
  2. $e^x$
  3. $\log x$
  4. $-\frac{1}{x^2}$
Explanation. $(\log x)' = \frac1x$; $-\frac1{x^2}$ is the derivative of $\frac1x$, $e^x$ is its own derivative.
am1-f-102 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of $f(x) = \sqrt{x} = x^{1/2}$ for $x > 0$?
  1. $\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}$
  2. $\frac{2}{3}\,x^{3/2}$
  3. $\frac{1}{2}\,\sqrt{x}$
  4. $\frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}$
Explanation. $(x^\alpha)' = \alpha\,x^{\alpha - 1}$ with $\alpha = \frac12$: $\frac12 x^{-1/2} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt x}$. $\frac23 x^{3/2}$ is an antiderivative, not the derivative.
am1-f-349 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of $f(x) = \cos x$?
  1. $\sin x$
  2. $-\sin x$
  3. $-\cos x$
  4. $\frac{1}{\cos^2 x}$
Explanation. $(\cos x)' = -\sin x$, while $(\sin x)' = \cos x$; $\frac{1}{\cos^2 x}$ is the derivative of $\tan x$.
am1-f-350 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of $f(x) = 2^x$?
  1. $x\,2^{x-1}$
  2. $2^x$
  3. $\frac{2^x}{\log 2}$
  4. $2^x \log 2$
Explanation. $2^x = e^{x\log 2}$, so $(2^x)' = \log 2 \cdot 2^x$ (natural $\log$). $x\,2^{x-1}$ wrongly applies the power rule.
am1-f-351 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^3}$ for $x \neq 0$?
  1. $-\frac{3}{x^4}$
  2. $\frac{3}{x^2}$
  3. $-\frac{1}{3x^2}$
  4. $-\frac{3}{x^2}$
Explanation. $x^{-3}$ has derivative $-3x^{-4} = -\frac{3}{x^4}$ (rule $(x^\alpha)' = \alpha x^{\alpha - 1}$ with $\alpha = -3$).
am1-f-352 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of $f(x) = \log_{10} x$ for $x > 0$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $\frac{1}{x}$
  2. $\frac{\log 10}{x}$
  3. $\frac{1}{x \log 10}$
  4. $\frac{10}{x}$
Explanation. $\log_{10} x = \frac{\log x}{\log 10}$, so the derivative is $\frac{1}{x\log 10}$.
am1-f-353 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of $f(x) = \sqrt[3]{x^2} = x^{2/3}$ for $x \neq 0$?
  1. $\frac{3}{2}\,x^{1/3}$
  2. $\frac{2}{3\sqrt[3]{x}}$
  3. $\frac{2}{3}\sqrt[3]{x}$
  4. $\frac{3}{5}\,x^{5/3}$
Explanation. $(x^{2/3})' = \frac23 x^{-1/3} = \frac{2}{3\sqrt[3]{x}}$. $\frac35 x^{5/3}$ is an antiderivative.
am1-f-354 · level teorema · notes
Which table of elementary derivatives is correct?
  1. $(e^x)' = e^x$, $(\sin x)' = \cos x$, $(\arcsin x)' = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}$
  2. $(e^x)' = x\,e^{x-1}$, $(\sin x)' = \cos x$, $(\arcsin x)' = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}$
  3. $(e^x)' = e^x$, $(\sin x)' = -\cos x$, $(\arcsin x)' = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}$
  4. $(e^x)' = e^x$, $(\sin x)' = \cos x$, $(\arcsin x)' = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}$
Explanation. The exponential equals its derivative, the derivative of sine is cosine and that of arcsine is $\frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}$ for $|x| < 1$ ($-\frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}$ is that of arccosine).
am1-f-599 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of $f(x) = \sin x$, and what is its value at the point $x = \pi$?
  1. $\cos x$, which equals $1$ at $\pi$
  2. $\cos x$, which equals $-1$ at $\pi$
  3. $-\cos x$, which equals $1$ at $\pi$
  4. $-\sin x$, which equals $0$ at $\pi$
Explanation. $(\sin x)' = \cos x$ and $\cos\pi = -1$: the tangent to the graph of the sine at $(\pi, 0)$ has slope $-1$.
am1-f-600 · level ipotesi · notes
One proves $(\sin x)' = \cos x$ by writing the difference quotient $\frac{\sin(x + h) - \sin x}{h} = \sin x \cdot \frac{\cos h - 1}{h} + \cos x \cdot \frac{\sin h}{h}$. Which limits as $h \rr 0$ are needed?
  1. $\frac{\sin h}{h} \rr 0$ and $\frac{1 - \cos h}{h} \rr 1$
  2. $\frac{e^h - 1}{h} \rr 1$ and $\frac{\log(1 + h)}{h} \rr 1$
  3. $\frac{\tan h}{h} \rr 1$ and $\frac{\sin h}{h} \rr 1$
  4. $\frac{\sin h}{h} \rr 1$ and $\frac{1 - \cos h}{h} \rr 0$
Explanation. With $\frac{\cos h - 1}{h} \rr 0$ and $\frac{\sin h}{h} \rr 1$ the quotient tends to $\sin x \cdot 0 + \cos x \cdot 1 = \cos x$.
am1-f-601 · level facile · notes
What are the derivatives of $f(x) = e^{x}$ and of $g(x) = \log|x|$ for $x \neq 0$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $e^x$ and $\frac{1}{|x|}$
  2. $x\,e^{x-1}$ and $\frac1x$
  3. $e^x$ and $\frac1x$
  4. $e^x$ and $-\frac1x$
Explanation. $(e^x)' = e^x$. For $x > 0$, $(\log x)' = \frac1x$; for $x < 0$, $(\log(-x))' = \frac{-1}{-x} = \frac1x$: in both cases $\frac1x$.
am1-f-602 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of the power function $f(x) = x^{\pi}$ for $x > 0$?
  1. $\pi\,x^{\pi - 1}$
  2. $x^\pi \log x$
  3. $\pi^x \log\pi$
  4. $\frac{x^{\pi + 1}}{\pi + 1}$
Explanation. $(x^\alpha)' = \alpha\,x^{\alpha - 1}$ for every real exponent $\alpha$. $\pi^x \log\pi$ is the derivative of the exponential $\pi^x$; the last is an antiderivative.

3. Corner points, cusps, points with vertical/horizontal tangent (12)

am1-f-103 · level facile · notes
The function $f(x) = |x - 1|$ at the point $x_0 = 1$ has:
  1. a corner point
  2. a cusp
  3. a point with vertical tangent
  4. a point with horizontal tangent
Explanation. $f$ is continuous, with right derivative $1$ and left derivative $-1$, finite and different: it is a corner point.
am1-f-104 · level facile · notes
The function $f(x) = \sqrt[3]{x}$ at the point $x_0 = 0$ has:
  1. a cusp
  2. a corner point
  3. a point with vertical tangent
  4. a point with horizontal tangent
Explanation. The difference quotient $\frac{\sqrt[3]{h}}{h} = h^{-2/3} \rr \ip$ from both sides: $f'(0) = \ip$. For a cusp the two limits would be infinite with opposite signs (as for $\sqrt{|x|}$).
am1-f-355 · level facile · notes
What are the left and right derivatives of $f(x) = |x^2 - 4|$ at $x_0 = 2$?
  1. $0$ and $0$
  2. $-2$ and $2$
  3. $-4$ and $4$
  4. $4$ and $-4$
Explanation. Near $2$: for $x < 2$, $f = 4 - x^2$ with derivative $-2x \rr -4$; for $x > 2$, $f = x^2 - 4$ with derivative $2x \rr 4$. It is a corner point.
am1-f-356 · level facile · notes
The function $f(x) = \sqrt{|x|}$ at the point $x_0 = 0$ has:
  1. a cusp
  2. a corner point
  3. a point with vertical tangent
  4. a point with horizontal tangent
Explanation. The difference quotient $\frac{\sqrt{|h|}}{h}$ tends to $\ip$ as $h \rr 0^+$ and to $\im$ as $h \rr 0^-$: infinite one-sided derivatives of opposite signs.
am1-f-357 · level facile · notes
A point $x_0$ where $f$ is continuous is called a corner point if:
  1. $f'_-(x_0) = f'_+(x_0)$
  2. the one-sided derivatives are infinite with opposite signs
  3. $f'(x_0) = 0$
  4. the one-sided derivatives exist, at least one is finite, and they differ
Explanation. At a corner the two half-tangents form an angle: left and right derivatives differ, not both infinite. With infinite derivatives of opposite sign there is a cusp.
am1-f-358 · level facile · notes
The function $f(x) = \sqrt[3]{x - 1}$ at the point $x_0 = 1$ has:
  1. a cusp
  2. a point with vertical tangent
  3. a corner point
  4. a discontinuity
Explanation. $\frac{\sqrt[3]{h}}{h} = h^{-2/3} \rr \ip$ from both sides: the derivative is $\ip$ and the tangent is the vertical line $x = 1$.
am1-f-359 · level facile · notes
What is the right derivative $f'_+(0)$ of $f(x) = |x|\,(x + 3)$?
  1. $3$
  2. $-3$
  3. $0$
  4. it does not exist
Explanation. For $x > 0$, $f(x) = x^2 + 3x$, with derivative $2x + 3 \rr 3$. The left derivative is $-3$: $0$ is a corner point.
am1-f-360 · level facile · notes
At how many points is the function $f(x) = |x^2 - 1|$ not differentiable?
  1. at none
  2. at one ($x = 0$)
  3. at two ($x = \pm 1$)
  4. at three ($x = 0, \pm 1$)
Explanation. Where $x^2 - 1 \neq 0$ the function is a polynomial. At $x = \pm1$ the polynomial has derivative $\pm 2 \neq 0$ and the absolute value creates two corners. At $0$ it is differentiable.
am1-f-603 · level facile · notes
A point $x_0$ at which $f$ is continuous is called a cusp if:
  1. the right and left derivatives are finite and different
  2. the right and left derivatives are infinite with opposite signs
  3. the right and left derivatives are infinite with the same sign
  4. $f'(x_0) = 0$
Explanation. At a cusp the one-sided tangents are vertical but the graph “turns back”. Finite different derivatives give a corner point, infinite ones of the same sign a point with vertical tangent.
am1-f-604 · level facile · notes
What kind of point is $x_0 = 0$ for the function $f(x) = \sqrt[3]{x^2}$?
  1. a cusp
  2. a point with vertical tangent
  3. a corner point
  4. a point of discontinuity
Explanation. $f'(x) = \frac{2}{3\sqrt[3]{x}}$ for $x \neq 0$: it tends to $\im$ from the left and to $\ip$ from the right, and $f$ is continuous at $0$.
am1-f-605 · level facile · notes
What are the left and right derivatives of $f(x) = \max\{x, x^2\}$ at the point $x_0 = 1$?
  1. $2$ and $1$
  2. $1$ and $1$
  3. $0$ and $2$
  4. $1$ and $2$
Explanation. Near $1$: for $x < 1$ we have $x > x^2$, so $f(x) = x$ with derivative $1$; for $x > 1$ we have $f(x) = x^2$ with derivative $2$. It is a corner point.
am1-f-606 · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = x^2$ for $x \le 1$ and $f(x) = a\,x - a + 1$ for $x > 1$. For which $a \in \R$ is $x = 1$ a corner point?
  1. only for $a = 2$
  2. for no $a$, because $f$ is discontinuous at $1$
  3. for every $a \neq 2$
  4. only for $a = 1$
Explanation. $f$ is continuous at $1$ for every $a$ (both pieces equal $1$). The left derivative is $2$, the right one $a$: the point is a corner if $a \neq 2$, while for $a = 2$ $f$ is differentiable.

4. Rules for computing derivatives (12)

am1-f-105 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of $f(x) = \tan x$?
  1. $-\frac{1}{\cos^2 x}$
  2. $1 + \tan^2 x$
  3. $\frac{1}{\sin^2 x}$
  4. $\frac{\cos x}{\sin x}$
Explanation. By the quotient rule: $\left(\frac{\sin x}{\cos x}\right)' = \frac{\cos^2 x + \sin^2 x}{\cos^2 x} = \frac{1}{\cos^2 x} = 1 + \tan^2 x$.
am1-f-106 · level ipotesi · notes
Theorem on the derivative of the inverse function $g = f^{-1}$: to conclude $g'(y_0) = \frac{1}{f'(x_0)}$ with $y_0 = f(x_0)$, which hypotheses are needed?
  1. $f$ continuous and invertible in $(a,b)$, $f'(x_0)$ exists and is $\neq 0$
  2. $f$ continuous and invertible in $(a,b)$ and $f'(x_0) = 0$
  3. $f$ invertible in $(a,b)$ (without continuity) and $f'(x_0)$ exists
  4. $f$ differentiable at $x_0$ (without invertibility)
Explanation. One needs a continuous inverse and $f'(x_0) \neq 0$. With $f(x) = x^3$ and $x_0 = 0$ we have $f'(0) = 0$, and indeed $g(y) = \sqrt[3]{y}$ is not differentiable at $0$.
am1-f-107 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of $f(y) = \arctan y$?
  1. $-\frac{1}{1 + y^2}$
  2. $\frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - y^2}}$
  3. $\frac{1}{1 + y^2}$
  4. $1 + y^2$
Explanation. With $y = \tan x$: $(\arctan y)' = \frac{1}{1 + \tan^2 x} = \frac{1}{1 + y^2}$. $\frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - y^2}}$ is the derivative of the arcsine.
am1-f-361 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of $f(x) = \sin(x^2)$?
  1. $\cos(x^2)$
  2. $2x\cos(x^2)$
  3. $2x\sin(x^2)$
  4. $\cos(2x)$
Explanation. Chain rule: $(\sin(g(x)))' = \cos(g(x))\,g'(x)$ with $g(x) = x^2$, $g'(x) = 2x$.
am1-f-362 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of $f(x) = x^2 e^x$?
  1. $2x\,e^x$
  2. $x^2 e^x$
  3. $2x\,e^{x-1}$
  4. $(x^2 + 2x)\,e^x$
Explanation. Product rule: $(x^2)'e^x + x^2 (e^x)' = 2x\,e^x + x^2 e^x$.
am1-f-363 · level facile · notes
Using the logarithmic derivative, what is the derivative of $f(x) = x^x$ for $x > 0$?
  1. $x^x(\log x + 1)$
  2. $x \cdot x^{x-1}$
  3. $x^x \log x$
  4. $x^x$
Explanation. $\log f = x\log x$, so $\frac{f'}{f} = \log x + 1$ and $f' = x^x(\log x + 1)$. Neither the power rule nor the exponential rule applies on its own.
am1-f-364 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of $f(x) = \frac{x}{x + 1}$ for $x \neq -1$?
  1. $\frac{1}{x + 1}$
  2. $\frac{2x + 1}{(x+1)^2}$
  3. $\frac{1}{(x + 1)^2}$
  4. $-\frac{1}{(x + 1)^2}$
Explanation. Quotient rule: $\frac{1 \cdot (x+1) - x \cdot 1}{(x+1)^2} = \frac{1}{(x+1)^2}$.
am1-f-365 · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = x^3 + x$ and $g = f^{-1}$ its inverse. What is $g'(2)$?
  1. $\frac{1}{13}$
  2. $\frac14$
  3. $4$
  4. $\frac{1}{2}$
Explanation. $f(1) = 2$, so $g'(2) = \frac{1}{f'(1)}$ with $f'(x) = 3x^2 + 1$, $f'(1) = 4$. $\frac1{13} = \frac1{f'(2)}$ uses the wrong point.
am1-f-607 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of $f(x) = \log(\cos x)$ at the points where $\cos x > 0$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $-\tan x$
  2. $\frac{1}{\cos x}$
  3. $\tan x$
  4. $-\frac{1}{\sin x}$
Explanation. Chain rule: $\frac{1}{\cos x} \cdot (-\sin x) = -\tan x$. The second option forgets the derivative of the inner function.
am1-f-608 · level facile · notes
The elasticity of $f$ at the point $x$ is $E_f(x) = \frac{x\,f'(x)}{f(x)}$. What is it for $f(x) = 5x^3$, $x > 0$?
  1. $15x^2$
  2. $3$
  3. $5$
  4. $\frac{3}{x}$
Explanation. $E_f(x) = \frac{x \cdot 15x^2}{5x^3} = 3$: power functions $c\,x^\alpha$ have constant elasticity $\alpha$. $15x^2$ is the derivative, not the elasticity.
am1-f-609 · level teorema · notes
Chain rule: if $g$ is differentiable at $x_0$ and $f$ is differentiable at $g(x_0)$, which formula holds?
  1. $(f \circ g)'(x_0) = f'(x_0)\,g'(x_0)$
  2. $(f \circ g)'(x_0) = f'(g(x_0)) + g'(x_0)$
  3. $(f \circ g)'(x_0) = f'\big(g'(x_0)\big)$
  4. $(f \circ g)'(x_0) = f'\big(g(x_0)\big)\,g'(x_0)$
Explanation. The derivative of the outer function is evaluated at $g(x_0)$ and multiplied by the derivative of the inner function. Example: $(\sin(x^2))' = \cos(x^2) \cdot 2x$.
am1-f-610 · level facile · notes
What is the derivative of $f(x) = \frac{e^x}{x}$ for $x \neq 0$?
  1. $\frac{e^x(1 - x)}{x^2}$
  2. $e^x$
  3. $\frac{e^x(x - 1)}{x^2}$
  4. $\frac{e^x}{x^2}$
Explanation. Quotient rule: $\frac{e^x \cdot x - e^x \cdot 1}{x^2} = \frac{e^x(x - 1)}{x^2}$. The first swaps the order of the terms in the numerator.

5. Mean value theorem, maxima and minima (12)

am1-f-108 · level teorema · notes
Which statement is Fermat's theorem?
  1. If $f'(x_0) = 0$, then $x_0$ is a local extremum point of $f$.
  2. If $f: [a,b] \rr \R$ has a local extremum at $x_0 \in [a,b]$, then $f'(x_0) = 0$.
  3. If $f: (a,b) \rr \R$ is differentiable at $x_0 \in (a,b)$ and has a local extremum at $x_0$, then $f'(x_0) = 0$.
  4. If $f$ is continuous at $x_0$ and has a local extremum at $x_0$, then $f'(x_0) = 0$.
Explanation. The point must be interior and $f$ differentiable there. $x^3$ has $f'(0) = 0$ with no extremum; $x$ on $[0,1]$ has a minimum at $0$ with $f' = 1$; $|x|$ has a minimum at $0$ with no derivative.
am1-f-109 · level ipotesi · notes
Lagrange's theorem for $f$ continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$: it is proved with the auxiliary function $w(x) = f(x) - \left(f(a) + \frac{f(b) - f(a)}{b - a}(x - a)\right)$. Which results are used?
  1. the intermediate zero theorem and the comparison theorem
  2. the Weierstrass theorem and Fermat's theorem
  3. L'Hôpital's rule
  4. Taylor's formula with Lagrange remainder
Explanation. $w(a) = w(b) = 0$; by Weierstrass $w$ has a maximum and a minimum in $[a,b]$; if it is not constant one of them is interior and there, by Fermat, $w'(c) = 0$.
am1-f-110 · level teorema · notes
Let $f: I \rr \R$ be continuous in $I$ and differentiable at the interior points of the interval $I$. Which statement is correct?
  1. If $f'(x) > 0$ at every interior point of $I$, then $f$ is increasing in $I$.
  2. If $f$ is increasing in $I$, then $f'(x) > 0$ at every interior point of $I$.
  3. If $f'(x) \ge 0$ at every interior point of $I$, then $f$ is increasing (strictly) in $I$.
  4. If $f'(x) \ge 0$ on a union of intervals, then $f$ is non-decreasing on the union.
Explanation. Differential monotonicity test (proved with Lagrange). $x^3$ is increasing with $f'(0) = 0$; a constant has $f' = 0$; $-\frac1x$ has $f' > 0$ on $\R\setminus\{0\}$ but is not monotone.
am1-f-111 · level facile · notes
For $f(x) = x^3$, the point $x_0 = 0$ is:
  1. a stationary point that is a local minimum
  2. a stationary point that is a local maximum
  3. a non-stationary point ($f'(0) \neq 0$)
  4. a stationary point that is not an extremum
Explanation. $f'(0) = 0$, so it is stationary; but $f$ is increasing on $\R$, so it is neither a local maximum nor a local minimum.
am1-f-366 · level facile · notes
Lagrange's theorem for $f(x) = x^2$ on $[1, 3]$: at which point $c \in (1, 3)$ is $f'(c) = \frac{f(3) - f(1)}{3 - 1}$?
  1. $c = \sqrt5$
  2. $c = \frac32$
  3. $c = 2$
  4. $c = 4$
Explanation. $\frac{9 - 1}{2} = 4$ and $f'(c) = 2c = 4$ gives $c = 2$. $4$ is the slope of the secant, not the point.
am1-f-367 · level facile · notes
What are the stationary points of $f(x) = x^3 - 12x$ and of which type?
  1. $x = -2$ local maximum, $x = 2$ local minimum
  2. $x = -2$ local minimum, $x = 2$ local maximum
  3. $x = 0$ inflection, no extremum
  4. $x = \pm 2\sqrt3$, both minima
Explanation. $f'(x) = 3x^2 - 12 = 3(x - 2)(x + 2)$ is positive outside $[-2, 2]$ and negative inside: $f$ increases, decreases, increases.
am1-f-368 · level ipotesi · notes
To conclude, by Lagrange's theorem, that there is $c \in (a, b)$ with $f'(c) = \frac{f(b) - f(a)}{b - a}$, which hypotheses on $f: [a, b] \rr \R$ are needed?
  1. $f$ differentiable on $(a, b)$ and $f(a) = f(b)$
  2. $f$ continuous on $[a, b]$ and monotone
  3. $f$ differentiable on $[a, b]$ with $f'$ continuous
  4. $f$ continuous on $[a, b]$ and differentiable on $(a, b)$
Explanation. Continuity on the closed interval and differentiability inside suffice. $f(a) = f(b)$ is the extra hypothesis of Rolle's theorem; $|x|$ on $[-1, 2]$ shows that without differentiability the thesis may fail.
am1-f-369 · level facile · notes
On which set is the function $f(x) = x\,e^{-x}$ (strictly) increasing?
  1. $[1, \ip)$
  2. $(-\infty, 1]$
  3. $(0, \ip)$
  4. $\R$
Explanation. $f'(x) = e^{-x}(1 - x) > 0$ for $x < 1$; by the monotonicity test $f$ is increasing on $(-\infty, 1]$.
am1-f-611 · level facile · notes
Let $f: D \rr \R$. The point $x_0 \in D$ is called a local maximum point of $f$ if:
  1. there is a neighborhood $U$ of $x_0$ with $f(x) \le f(x_0)$ for every $x \in U \cap D$
  2. $f(x) \le f(x_0)$ for every $x \in D$
  3. $f'(x_0) = 0$ and $f''(x_0) < 0$
  4. there is a neighborhood $U$ of $x_0$ with $f(x) \ge f(x_0)$ for every $x \in U \cap D$
Explanation. The inequality need only hold near $x_0$. The second is the definition of absolute maximum, the third a sufficient condition (for twice differentiable $f$), the fourth defines a local minimum.
am1-f-612 · level ipotesi · notes
For $f(x) = |x|$ the point $x_0 = 0$ is a minimum, but $f'(0)$ does not exist. Why does this not contradict Fermat's theorem?
  1. because the theorem concerns only maximum points
  2. because the theorem requires $f$ to be differentiable at $x_0$
  3. because the theorem requires a closed interval
  4. because the theorem requires $f$ to be twice differentiable
Explanation. Fermat: if $f$ has a local extremum at an interior point $x_0$ and $f$ is differentiable at $x_0$, then $f'(x_0) = 0$. Here the differentiability hypothesis is missing.
am1-f-613 · level facile · notes
What are the absolute maximum and minimum of $f(x) = x^3 - 3x$ on the interval $[0, 2]$?
  1. maximum $0$, minimum $-2$
  2. maximum $2$, minimum $0$
  3. maximum $2$, minimum $-2$
  4. maximum $2$, minimum $-3$
Explanation. Compare the endpoints $f(0) = 0$, $f(2) = 2$ and the interior stationary point $x = 1$ ($f'(x) = 3x^2 - 3$), where $f(1) = -2$.
am1-f-614 · level facile · notes
Let $f$ be differentiable on $\R$ with $f'(x) = (x - 1)^2\,(x + 2)$. On which maximal interval is $f$ (strictly) increasing?
  1. $[1, \ip)$
  2. $[-2, 1]$
  3. $(-\infty, -2]$
  4. $[-2, \ip)$
Explanation. $f' > 0$ for $x > -2$, except at $x = 1$ where $f' = 0$: an isolated point where the derivative vanishes does not prevent strict increase. On $(-\infty, -2]$ $f$ is decreasing.

6. L'Hôpital's rule and differentiability (12)

am1-f-112 · level ipotesi · notes
L'Hôpital's rule as $x \rr a^+$: which hypotheses are needed to conclude $\frac{f(x)}{g(x)} \rr \ell$?
  1. $f, g$ differentiable on $(a,b)$, $g, g' \neq 0$, $f, g \rr 0$ (or $\pm\infty$) and $\frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)} \rr \ell \in \R \cup \{\pm\infty\}$
  2. $f, g$ differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $f, g \rr 0$; the limit of $\frac{f'}{g'}$ need not exist
  3. $f, g$ differentiable on $(a,b)$, $g, g' \neq 0$ and $\frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)} \rr \ell$, even if $f \rr 1$ and $g \rr 2$
  4. $f, g$ continuous on $[a,b)$ and $f(a) = g(a) = 0$
Explanation. One needs the form $\left[\frac00\right]$ or $\left[\frac\infty\infty\right]$ and the existence of the limit of $\frac{f'}{g'}$. With $f \rr 1$, $g \rr 2$ the limit is $\frac12$ whatever $\frac{f'}{g'}$ does.
am1-f-113 · level teorema · notes
Limit of the derivative theorem: which statement is correct?
  1. If $f$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $\lim_{x \rr a^+} f'(x) = m$, then $f'_+(a) = m$, even if $f$ is not continuous at $a$.
  2. If $f: [a,b) \rr \R$ is continuous at $a$, differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $\lim_{x \rr a^+} f'(x) = m \in \R \cup \{\pm\infty\}$, then $f'_+(a) = m$.
  3. If $f'_+(a)$ exists, then $\lim_{x \rr a^+} f'(x)$ exists and equals $f'_+(a)$.
  4. If $f'_+(a) = m$, then $f'$ is continuous at $a$.
Explanation. Continuity at $a$ is needed: a function with a jump at $a$ and $f' \equiv 0$ has no right derivative $0$. The converse is false: $x^2\sin\frac1x$ is differentiable at $0$ but $f'$ has no limit.
am1-f-114 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{x - \sin x}{x^3}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $-\frac16$
  3. $\frac16$
  4. $\frac13$
Explanation. By L'Hôpital: $\frac{1 - \cos x}{3x^2} \rr \frac{1}{3}\cdot\frac12 = \frac16$. Using only $\sin x = x + o(x)$ leaves the indeterminate form $\frac{o(x)}{x^3}$.
am1-f-370 · level facile · notes
Using L'Hôpital's rule, what is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \frac{\log x}{\sqrt x}$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $0$
  2. $1$
  3. $2$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. Form $\left[\frac\infty\infty\right]$: $\frac{1/x}{1/(2\sqrt x)} = \frac{2}{\sqrt x} \rr 0$.
am1-f-371 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{e^x - 1 - x}{x^2}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $1$
  3. $\frac12$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. L'Hôpital twice: $\frac{e^x - 1}{2x}$, then $\frac{e^x}{2} \rr \frac12$.
am1-f-372 · level facile · notes
Why can L'Hôpital's rule not be applied to $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{x + 1}{x + 2}$?
  1. because the functions are not differentiable at $0$
  2. because the limit of $\frac{f'}{g'}$ does not exist
  3. because $g'(x) = 1$ does not depend on $x$
  4. because it is not an indeterminate form ($\frac12$)
Explanation. L'Hôpital's rule requires a form $\left[\frac00\right]$ or $\left[\frac\infty\infty\right]$; here the limit is simply $\frac12$. Applying it anyway would give $\frac11 = 1$, which is wrong.
am1-f-373 · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = x^2$ for $x \le 0$ and $f(x) = \sin x$ for $x > 0$. By the limit of the derivative theorem, what is $f'_+(0)$?
  1. $0$
  2. $1$
  3. it does not exist
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. $f$ is continuous at $0$ ($f(0) = 0 = \lim_{x \rr 0^+} \sin x$) and $f'(x) = \cos x \rr 1$ as $x \rr 0^+$: hence $f'_+(0) = 1$, while $f'_-(0) = 0$.
am1-f-374 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 0^+} x^2 \log x$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $0$
  2. $\im$
  3. $1$
  4. $-\frac12$
Explanation. Write it as $\frac{\log x}{x^{-2}}$, form $\left[\frac{\infty}{\infty}\right]$: $\frac{1/x}{-2x^{-3}} = -\frac{x^2}{2} \rr 0$.
am1-f-615 · level facile · notes
Using L'Hôpital's rule, what is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{\sin x - x}{x^2}$?
  1. $-\frac16$
  2. $0$
  3. $\frac12$
  4. $-1$
Explanation. Form $\left[\frac00\right]$: the ratio of the derivatives is $\frac{\cos x - 1}{2x} \rr 0$ (since $1 - \cos x \sim \frac{x^2}{2}$). The value $-\frac16$ is the limit with $x^3$ in the denominator.
am1-f-616 · level facile · notes
Using L'Hôpital's rule, what is $\lim_{x \rr 1} \frac{\log x}{x - 1}$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $0$
  2. $\ip$
  3. $1$
  4. $e$
Explanation. Form $\left[\frac00\right]$: the ratio of the derivatives is $\frac{1/x}{1} \rr 1$.
am1-f-617 · level teorema · notes
Let $f(x) = x^2 \sin\frac1x$ for $x \neq 0$ and $f(0) = 0$. Then $f'(0) = 0$, but $\lim_{x \rr 0} f'(x)$ does not exist. What does the example show about the theorem on the limit of the derivative?
  1. that $f$ is not continuous at $0$
  2. that the theorem is false
  3. that $f'(0)$ does not actually exist
  4. that the theorem gives a sufficient but not necessary condition
Explanation. The theorem says: if $f$ is continuous at $x_0$ and $f'(x) \rr m$, then $f'(x_0) = m$. The converse fails: here $f'(0) = \lim_{h \rr 0} h \sin\frac1h = 0$ exists, while $f'(x) = 2x\sin\frac1x - \cos\frac1x$ oscillates.
am1-f-618 · level facile · notes
For which indeterminate form does one rewrite $f(x)\,g(x) = \frac{f(x)}{1/g(x)}$ before applying L'Hôpital's rule?
  1. $[0 \cdot \infty]$
  2. $[\ip - \infty]$
  3. $[1^\infty]$
  4. $\left[\frac00\right]$
Explanation. If $f \rr 0$ and $g \rr \infty$, the quotient $\frac{f}{1/g}$ has the form $\left[\frac00\right]$, to which the rule applies. The form $\left[\frac00\right]$ is already ready for the rule.

7. Second derivative (12)

am1-f-115 · level teorema · notes
Let $f: (a,b) \rr \R$ be twice differentiable on $(a,b)$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $f$ is convex on $(a,b)$ if and only if $f''(x) > 0$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
  2. $f$ is convex on $(a,b)$ if and only if $f''(x) \ge 0$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
  3. $f$ is convex on $(a,b)$ if and only if $f'$ is decreasing on $(a,b)$.
  4. $f$ is convex on $(a,b)$ if and only if $f'(x) \ge 0$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
Explanation. A differentiable function is convex if and only if $f'$ is non-decreasing, i.e. (monotonicity test applied to $f'$) $f'' \ge 0$. $x^4$ is convex with $f''(0) = 0$; $e^{-x}$ is convex and decreasing.
am1-f-116 · level facile · notes
What are the inflection points of $f(x) = x^4 - 6x^2$?
  1. $x = -1$ and $x = 1$
  2. $x = 0$
  3. $x = -\sqrt3$ and $x = \sqrt3$
  4. none
Explanation. $f''(x) = 12x^2 - 12$ vanishes and changes sign at $x = \pm1$. $x = 0$ is a stationary point (local maximum), $\pm\sqrt3$ are minima.
am1-f-117 · level facile · notes
Definition of convexity: a function $f: I \rr \R$, with $I$ an interval, is convex on $I$ if:
  1. the set $\{(x,y): x \in I,~ y = f(x)\}$ is convex
  2. the set $\{(x,y): x \in I,~ y \ge f'(x)\}$ is convex
  3. the set $\{(x,y): x \in I,~ y \le f(x)\}$ is convex
  4. the set $\{(x,y): x \in I,~ y \ge f(x)\}$ is convex
Explanation. $f$ is convex if its epigraph $\epi f = \{(x,y): y \ge f(x)\}$ is convex. If the set below the graph is convex, the function is concave; the graph of a non-linear function is not a convex set.
am1-f-375 · level facile · notes
What is the third derivative of $f(x) = x^4 - 2x^3$ at $x = 1$?
  1. $0$
  2. $-4$
  3. $24$
  4. $12$
Explanation. $f' = 4x^3 - 6x^2$, $f'' = 12x^2 - 12x$, $f''' = 24x - 12$; at $x = 1$: $12$.
am1-f-376 · level facile · notes
On which set is the function $f(x) = x^3 - 3x^2$ convex?
  1. $(-\infty, 1]$
  2. $[1, \ip)$
  3. $[0, 2]$
  4. $\R$
Explanation. $f''(x) = 6x - 6 \ge 0 \iff x \ge 1$. A twice differentiable function is convex where $f'' \ge 0$.
am1-f-377 · level teorema · notes
Let $f$ be differentiable and convex on an open interval $I$, and $x_0 \in I$. Which statement is correct?
  1. The graph of $f$ lies above the tangent line at $x_0$: $f(x) \ge f(x_0) + f'(x_0)(x - x_0)$ for every $x \in I$.
  2. The graph of $f$ lies below the tangent line at $x_0$.
  3. The graph of $f$ crosses the tangent line at $x_0$.
  4. The tangent line at $x_0$ is horizontal.
Explanation. Differentiable convex functions lie above all their tangents (e.g. $e^x \ge 1 + x$). The graph crosses the tangent at inflection points.
am1-f-378 · level facile · notes
The function $f(x) = x^4$ has $f''(0) = 0$. Is $x = 0$ an inflection point?
  1. yes, because $f''(0) = 0$
  2. yes, because $f'(0) = 0$ and $f''(0) = 0$
  3. no, because $f''(x) = 12x^2$ does not change sign at $0$
  4. no, because $f$ is not twice differentiable at $0$
Explanation. An inflection requires a change of convexity; $f''(0) = 0$ is only a necessary condition (for twice differentiable $f$).
am1-f-379 · level facile · notes
A set $C \subseteq \R^2$ is convex if it contains the segment joining any two of its points. Which set is convex?
  1. the circle $\{(x,y): x^2 + y^2 = 1\}$
  2. $\{(x,y): y \le x^2\}$
  3. $\{(x,y): xy \ge 1\}$
  4. the disc $\{(x,y): x^2 + y^2 \le 1\}$
Explanation. The closed disc is convex. On the circle the segment between two points leaves the set; $y \le x^2$ (below a parabola) and $xy \ge 1$ do not contain the segment between $(-1,1)$ and $(1,1)$, or between $(1,1)$ and $(-1,-1)$.
am1-f-619 · level facile · notes
What is the second derivative of $f(x) = e^{2x}$?
  1. $2e^{2x}$
  2. $4e^{2x}$
  3. $e^{2x}$
  4. $4x\,e^{2x}$
Explanation. $f'(x) = 2e^{2x}$ and $f''(x) = 4e^{2x}$: each differentiation brings a factor $2$ by the chain rule.
am1-f-620 · level facile · notes
The tangent line to the graph of $f(x) = e^x$ at $x_0 = 0$ is $y = 1 + x$. Since $e^x$ is convex on $\R$, which inequality holds?
  1. $e^x \le 1 + x$ for every $x \in \R$
  2. $e^x \ge 1 + x$ only for $x \ge 0$
  3. $e^x \ge 1 + x$ for every $x \in \R$
  4. $e^x \le 1 + x + \frac{x^2}{2}$ for every $x \in \R$
Explanation. The graph of a convex differentiable function lies above each of its tangent lines. The last one fails for $x > 0$ (there $e^x$ exceeds even $1 + x + \frac{x^2}{2}$).
am1-f-621 · level facile · notes
Where is the function $f(x) = \log(1 + x^2)$ (natural $\log$) concave?
  1. on $[-1, 1]$
  2. on all of $\R$
  3. on $[0, \ip)$
  4. on $(-\infty, -1]$ and on $[1, \ip)$
Explanation. $f'(x) = \frac{2x}{1 + x^2}$ and $f''(x) = \frac{2(1 - x^2)}{(1 + x^2)^2}$, which is $\le 0$ for $|x| \ge 1$. On $[-1, 1]$ the function is convex.
am1-f-622 · level facile · notes
What is the point $x_0 = 0$ for the function $f(x) = x^3 + x$?
  1. an inflection point, with tangent $y = x$
  2. an inflection point with horizontal tangent
  3. a local minimum point
  4. an ordinary point: not an inflection since $f'(0) \neq 0$
Explanation. $f''(x) = 6x$ changes sign at $0$, so $0$ is an inflection point; the tangent has slope $f'(0) = 1$, i.e. it is $y = x$. An inflection point does not require $f'(x_0) = 0$.

8. Differential calculus and approximations (12)

am1-f-118 · level teorema · notes
Taylor's formula with Lagrange remainder: let $f$ be $n+1$ times differentiable on $[a,b]$ and $x_0 \in [a,b]$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $f(x) = T_{n,f,x_0}(x) + \frac{f^{(n+1)}(x_0)}{(n+1)!}(x - x_0)^{n+1}$
  2. there exists $c$ between $x_0$ and $x$ with $f(x) = T_{n,f,x_0}(x) + \frac{f^{(n)}(c)}{n!}(x - x_0)^{n}$
  3. there exists $c$ between $x_0$ and $x$ with $f(x) = T_{n,f,x_0}(x) + \frac{f^{(n+1)}(c)}{(n+1)!}(x - x_0)^{n+1}$
  4. $f(x) = T_{n,f,x_0}(x) + o\big((x - x_0)^{n+1}\big)$ as $x \rr x_0$
Explanation. The remainder has the form of the next term of the polynomial, but with the derivative evaluated at an intermediate point $c$, not at $x_0$.
am1-f-119 · level facile · notes
The differential of $f$ at the point $x_0$ (increment along the tangent line) is:
  1. $df(x_0) = f'(x_0)\,dx$
  2. $df(x_0) = f(x_0)\,dx$
  3. $df(x_0) = f(x_0 + dx) - f(x_0)$
  4. $df(x_0) = f'(x_0) + dx$
Explanation. Along the tangent, with slope $f'(x_0)$, the increment is $f'(x_0)\,dx$. $f(x_0 + dx) - f(x_0)$ is the increment of the function, which differs from the differential by an $o(dx)$.
am1-f-120 · level facile · notes
What is the Maclaurin polynomial of degree $2$ of $f(x) = \cos x$?
  1. $1 + \frac{x^2}{2}$
  2. $1 - \frac{x^2}{2}$
  3. $1 - x^2$
  4. $x - \frac{x^2}{2}$
Explanation. $f(0) = 1$, $f'(0) = -\sin 0 = 0$, $f''(0) = -\cos 0 = -1$: $T_{2}(x) = 1 - \frac{x^2}{2!}$.
am1-f-380 · level facile · notes
What is the MacLaurin polynomial of degree $3$ of $f(x) = e^x$?
  1. $1 + x + x^2 + x^3$
  2. $1 + x + \frac{x^2}{2} + \frac{x^3}{6}$
  3. $x + \frac{x^2}{2} + \frac{x^3}{6}$
  4. $1 + x + \frac{x^2}{2} + \frac{x^3}{3}$
Explanation. All derivatives of $e^x$ at $0$ equal $1$, so the coefficients are $\frac{1}{k!}$: $1, 1, \frac12, \frac16$.
am1-f-381 · level facile · notes
What is the Taylor polynomial of degree $2$ of $f(x) = \log x$ (natural $\log$) centered at $x_0 = 1$?
  1. $(x - 1) + \frac{(x-1)^2}{2}$
  2. $1 + (x - 1) - \frac{(x-1)^2}{2}$
  3. $x - \frac{x^2}{2}$
  4. $(x - 1) - \frac{(x-1)^2}{2}$
Explanation. $f(1) = 0$, $f'(1) = 1$, $f''(1) = -1$: $T_2(x) = (x-1) - \frac{(x-1)^2}{2}$.
am1-f-382 · level facile · notes
If the MacLaurin polynomial of degree $4$ of $f$ is $2 - x + 3x^4$, what is $f^{(4)}(0)$?
  1. $72$
  2. $3$
  3. $12$
  4. $24$
Explanation. The coefficient of $x^k$ is $\frac{f^{(k)}(0)}{k!}$: $\frac{f^{(4)}(0)}{24} = 3$, hence $f^{(4)}(0) = 72$.
am1-f-383 · level ipotesi · notes
To conclude that $f(x) = T_{n}(x) + o\big((x - x_0)^n\big)$ as $x \rr x_0$ (Taylor's formula with Peano remainder, $T_n$ the Taylor polynomial of degree $n$ centered at $x_0$), which hypothesis on $f$ suffices?
  1. $f$ continuous in a neighborhood of $x_0$
  2. $f$ differentiable once at $x_0$, for every $n$
  3. $f$ differentiable $n$ times at $x_0$
  4. $f$ with bounded $(n+1)$-th derivative on all of $\R$
Explanation. With the Peano remainder the existence of $f^{(n)}(x_0)$ suffices (proved by applying L'Hôpital $n - 1$ times). The $(n+1)$-th derivative is needed for the Lagrange remainder.
am1-f-384 · level facile · notes
We approximate $\sin x$ by $x$ for $x \in [0, 0.1]$. With the Lagrange remainder of the degree-$2$ polynomial ($T_2(x) = x$), which bound on the error $|\sin x - x|$ is correct?
  1. $\le 0.1$
  2. $\le \frac{(0.1)^3}{6}$
  3. $\le \frac{(0.1)^2}{2}$
  4. $= 0$
Explanation. The remainder is $\frac{f'''(c)}{3!}x^3$ with $|f'''(c)| = |\cos c| \le 1$, so the error is at most $\frac{(0.1)^3}{6} \approx 1.7 \cdot 10^{-4}$.
am1-f-623 · level facile · notes
As $x \rr 0$, which first-order expansion (i.e. with the tangent line at $0$) of $f(x) = \sqrt{1 + 4x}$ is correct?
  1. $1 + 4x + o(x)$
  2. $1 + 2x + o(x)$
  3. $1 + \frac{x}{4} + o(x)$
  4. $2x + o(x)$
Explanation. $f(0) = 1$ and $f'(x) = \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + 4x}}$, so $f'(0) = 2$: $f(x) = 1 + 2x + o(x)$ (also from $(1 + t)^{1/2} - 1 \sim \frac t2$ with $t = 4x$).
am1-f-624 · level facile · notes
Let $P(x) = 2 - x + 5x^3$. What is the third derivative $P'''(0)$?
  1. $5$
  2. $15$
  3. $30$
  4. $0$
Explanation. $P'''(x) = 5 \cdot 3 \cdot 2 = 30$ (constant). In general the coefficient of $x^k$ is $\frac{P^{(k)}(0)}{k!}$: $5 = \frac{30}{3!}$.
am1-f-625 · level teorema · notes
Let $P$ be a polynomial of degree $n$. Which formula expresses $P$ through its derivatives at the point $0$?
  1. $P(x) = \sum_{k=0}^{n} P^{(k)}(0)\,x^k$
  2. $P(x) = \sum_{k=0}^{n} \frac{P^{(k)}(0)}{k}\,x^k$
  3. $P(x) = \sum_{k=0}^{n} \frac{P^{(k)}(1)}{k!}\,x^k$
  4. $P(x) = \sum_{k=0}^{n} \frac{P^{(k)}(0)}{k!}\,x^k$
Explanation. Differentiating $\sum_j c_j x^j$ $k$ times and setting $x = 0$ leaves only $k!\,c_k$, so $c_k = \frac{P^{(k)}(0)}{k!}$: a polynomial coincides with its own Maclaurin polynomial of degree $n$.
am1-f-626 · level facile · notes
What is the Maclaurin polynomial of degree $3$ of $f(x) = \sin(2x)$?
  1. $2x - \frac43 x^3$
  2. $2x - \frac{x^3}{6}$
  3. $2x - \frac83 x^3$
  4. $x - \frac{x^3}{6}$
Explanation. From $\sin t = t - \frac{t^3}{6} + o(t^3)$ with $t = 2x$: $2x - \frac{8x^3}{6} = 2x - \frac43 x^3$. The second forgets to cube the factor $2$.

9. Curve sketching (12)

am1-f-121 · level facile · notes
For $f(x) = x\,e^{-x}$ on $\R$, which statement is correct?
  1. $x = 1$ is the global maximum point and $f(1) = \frac1e$
  2. $x = 1$ is the global minimum point and $f(1) = \frac1e$
  3. $x = 0$ is the global maximum point
  4. $x = -1$ is the global maximum point and $f(-1) = -e$
Explanation. $f'(x) = e^{-x}(1 - x)$: $f$ increases for $x < 1$ and decreases for $x > 1$; since $f \rr \im$ as $x \rr \im$ and $f \rr 0$ as $x \rr \ip$, $f(1) = \frac1e$ is the maximum.
am1-f-122 · level facile · notes
Which asymptotes does $f(x) = \frac{x^2 + 1}{x}$ have?
  1. vertical asymptote $x = 0$ and horizontal asymptote $y = 1$
  2. oblique asymptote $y = x + 1$
  3. vertical asymptote $x = 0$ and oblique asymptote $y = x$
  4. no asymptotes
Explanation. $f(x) = x + \frac1x$: as $x \rr 0^\pm$ $f \rr \pm\infty$, and $f(x) - x = \frac1x \rr 0$ as $x \rr \pm\infty$.
am1-f-385 · level facile · notes
Which curve-sketching summary matches $f(x) = \frac{x}{x^2 + 1}$?
  1. odd, vertical asymptote $x = 1$, maximum at $x = 0$
  2. even, horizontal asymptote $y = 1$
  3. odd, horizontal asymptote $y = 0$, maximum at $x = 1$ and minimum at $x = -1$
  4. odd, oblique asymptote $y = x$
Explanation. $f(-x) = -f(x)$; $f \rr 0$ as $x \rr \pm\infty$; $f'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(x^2 + 1)^2}$ vanishes at $\pm1$, with $f(1) = \frac12$ maximum and $f(-1) = -\frac12$ minimum. The denominator never vanishes.
am1-f-386 · level facile · notes
How many real zeros does $f(x) = x^3 - 3x + 1$ have? (Hint: $f(-1) = 3$ is the local maximum, $f(1) = -1$ the local minimum.)
  1. $3$
  2. $1$
  3. $2$
  4. $0$
Explanation. $f \rr \im$ as $x \rr \im$, rises to $3 > 0$, falls to $-1 < 0$, then tends to $\ip$: by the intermediate zero theorem and piecewise monotonicity there are three zeros.
am1-f-387 · level facile · notes
What is the absolute minimum of $f(x) = x \log x$ on $(0, \ip)$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $0$, at $x = 1$
  2. it does not exist
  3. $-1$, at $x = e$
  4. $-\frac1e$, at $x = \frac1e$
Explanation. $f'(x) = \log x + 1 = 0$ for $x = \frac1e$; $f$ decreases before and increases after, so the minimum is $f\left(\frac1e\right) = -\frac1e$.
am1-f-388 · level facile · notes
What is the domain of $f(x) = \frac{e^x}{x - 1}$ and which asymptotes does it have?
  1. $\R$; horizontal asymptote $y = 0$ as $x \rr \im$
  2. $\R \setminus \{1\}$; asymptotes $x = 1$ and $y = 0$ (as $x \rr \im$)
  3. $\R \setminus \{1\}$; asymptotes $x = 1$ and $y = x$ (oblique)
  4. $(1, \ip)$; asymptotes $x = 1$ and $y = 0$ (as $x \rr \ip$)
Explanation. The denominator vanishes at $1$, where $f \rr \pm\infty$. As $x \rr \im$, $e^x \rr 0$ and $f \rr 0$; as $x \rr \ip$ the exponential dominates and $f \rr \ip$ with no oblique asymptote.
am1-f-389 · level facile · notes
For $f(x) = e^{-x^2}$, what are the inflection points?
  1. $x = 0$
  2. $x = \pm 1$
  3. $x = \pm\frac{1}{\sqrt2}$
  4. there are no inflection points
Explanation. $f'(x) = -2x e^{-x^2}$, $f''(x) = (4x^2 - 2)e^{-x^2}$, which changes sign at $x = \pm\frac{1}{\sqrt2}$. At $0$ there is the maximum.
am1-f-390 · level facile · notes
For $f(x) = \frac{x^2}{x - 1}$, what is the oblique asymptote as $x \rr \pm\infty$?
  1. $y = x + 1$
  2. $y = x$
  3. $y = x - 1$
  4. $y = 2x$
Explanation. $\frac{x^2}{x - 1} = x + 1 + \frac{1}{x - 1}$ (polynomial division): $f(x) - (x + 1) \rr 0$.
am1-f-627 · level facile · notes
What is the oblique asymptote of $f(x) = \sqrt{x^2 + 2x}$ as $x \rr \ip$?
  1. $y = x$
  2. $y = x + 2$
  3. $y = 2x$
  4. $y = x + 1$
Explanation. $m = \lim \frac{f(x)}{x} = 1$ and $q = \lim \left(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x\right) = \lim \frac{2x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x} = 1$.
am1-f-628 · level facile · notes
Sketching $f(x) = x^2 e^{-x}$ on $\R$: what are its local extrema?
  1. local maximum at $0$ ($f = 0$), local minimum at $2$ ($f = \frac{4}{e^2}$)
  2. local minimum at $0$ ($f = 0$), local maximum at $2$ ($f = \frac{4}{e^2}$)
  3. local minimum at $0$ ($f = 0$), local maximum at $1$ ($f = \frac1e$)
  4. local minimum at $0$ ($f = 0$), no local maximum
Explanation. $f'(x) = x(2 - x)e^{-x}$: negative for $x < 0$, positive on $(0, 2)$, negative for $x > 2$. Hence a minimum at $0$ and a maximum at $2$, with $f(2) = 4e^{-2}$.
am1-f-629 · level facile · notes
Sketching $f(x) = \frac{\log x}{x}$ on $(0, \ip)$ (natural $\log$): where is its absolute maximum and what is its value?
  1. at $x = e$, with value $\frac1e$
  2. at $x = 1$, with value $0$
  3. at $x = \frac1e$, with value $-e$
  4. it has no maximum, because $f \rr \ip$
Explanation. $f'(x) = \frac{1 - \log x}{x^2}$ is positive for $x < e$ and negative for $x > e$: absolute maximum $f(e) = \frac1e$. Moreover $f \rr \im$ as $x \rr 0^+$ and $f \rr 0$ as $x \rr \ip$.
am1-f-630 · level facile · notes
How many real zeros does $f(x) = x^4 - 4x + 5$ have?
  1. one
  2. two
  3. none
  4. four
Explanation. $f'(x) = 4x^3 - 4$ vanishes only at $x = 1$, which is the absolute minimum ($f$ decreases, then increases); $f(1) = 2 > 0$, so $f > 0$ everywhere.

10. Newton's method (12)

am1-f-123 · level ipotesi · notes
Newton's method theorem with starting point $x_0 = a$: $f$ twice differentiable on $[a,b]$. Which hypotheses are needed for convergence (from below) to the zero?
  1. $f(a)\,f(b) < 0$, $f'$ and $f''$ with constant sign on $[a,b]$, $f(a)\,f''(a) > 0$
  2. $f(a)\,f(b) < 0$, $f'$ and $f''$ with constant sign on $[a,b]$, $f(b)\,f''(b) > 0$
  3. only $f(a)\,f(b) < 0$
  4. $f'$ with constant sign on $[a,b]$ and $f(a) > 0$
Explanation. With $f(b)f''(b) > 0$ one starts instead from $x_0 = b$ and converges from above. The condition $f(a)f(b) < 0$ alone guarantees a zero, not the convergence of the method.
am1-f-124 · level facile · notes
Newton's method for $f(x) = x^2 - 2$ with $x_0 = 2$: what is $x_1$?
  1. $1$
  2. $\frac32$
  3. $\frac74$
  4. $\frac52$
Explanation. $x_1 = x_0 - \frac{f(x_0)}{f'(x_0)} = 2 - \frac{2}{4} = \frac32$. $\frac52$ comes from a sign error.
am1-f-391 · level facile · notes
Newton's method: which formula gives the iterate $x_{n+1}$ for solving $f(x) = 0$?
  1. $x_{n+1} = x_n - f(x_n)\,f'(x_n)$
  2. $x_{n+1} = x_n + \frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}$
  3. $x_{n+1} = \frac{x_n + f(x_n)}{2}$
  4. $x_{n+1} = x_n - \frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}$
Explanation. $x_{n+1}$ is where the tangent at $(x_n, f(x_n))$, $y = f(x_n) + f'(x_n)(x - x_n)$, meets the $x$-axis.
am1-f-392 · level facile · notes
Newton's method for $f(x) = x^2 - 3$ with $x_0 = 2$: what is $x_1$?
  1. $\frac32$
  2. $\frac74$
  3. $\frac94$
  4. $\frac53$
Explanation. $x_1 = 2 - \frac{4 - 3}{2 \cdot 2} = 2 - \frac14 = \frac74 = 1.75$ (while $\sqrt3 \approx 1.732$).
am1-f-393 · level facile · notes
Applied to $f(x) = x^2 - b$ ($b > 0$), Newton's method coincides with:
  1. Heron's algorithm $x_{n+1} = \frac12\left(x_n + \frac{b}{x_n}\right)$
  2. the bisection method on the interval $[0, b]$
  3. the sequence $x_{n+1} = \frac{x_n}{2} + \frac{b}{2}$
  4. the sequence $x_{n+1} = x_n - (x_n^2 - b)$
Explanation. $x_n - \frac{x_n^2 - b}{2x_n} = \frac{x_n}{2} + \frac{b}{2x_n} = \frac12\left(x_n + \frac b{x_n}\right)$.
am1-f-394 · level facile · notes
Why can Newton's method for $f(x) = x^3 - 3x$ not be started from $x_0 = 1$?
  1. because $f(1) = 0$, i.e. $1$ is already a zero
  2. because $f$ is not differentiable at $1$
  3. because $f'(1) = 0$ (horizontal tangent)
  4. because $f''(1) = 0$ (inflection at $1$)
Explanation. $f'(x) = 3x^2 - 3$ vanishes at $1$: the formula $x_1 = x_0 - \frac{f(x_0)}{f'(x_0)}$ would divide by $0$ (the horizontal tangent does not meet the $x$-axis). Moreover $f(1) = -2 \neq 0$ and $f''(1) = 6 \neq 0$.
am1-f-395 · level teorema · notes
Under the hypotheses of the convergence theorem for Newton's method ($f'$ and $f''$ of constant sign near the zero $\bar x$), how does the error $e_n = |x_n - \bar x|$ behave?
  1. $e_{n+1} \le \frac12 e_n$ (linear convergence)
  2. $e_{n+1} \le C\,e_n^2$ for a constant $C$ (quadratic convergence)
  3. $e_n$ stays constant
  4. $e_{n+1} \le \frac{e_n}{n}$
Explanation. From Taylor's formula with Lagrange remainder, $e_{n+1} \le \frac{\max|f''|}{2\min|f'|}\,e_n^2$: the number of correct digits roughly doubles at each step. Bisection instead has $e_{n+1} \le \frac12 e_n$.
am1-f-396 · level facile · notes
Newton's method for $f(x) = x^3 - 2$ with $x_0 = 1$: what is $x_1$?
  1. $\frac32$
  2. $\frac54$
  3. $\frac53$
  4. $\frac43$
Explanation. $f(1) = -1$, $f'(1) = 3$: $x_1 = 1 - \frac{-1}{3} = \frac43 \approx 1.33$ (while $\sqrt[3]{2} \approx 1.26$).
am1-f-631 · level facile · notes
Newton's method converges quadratically: $e_{n+1} \approx e_n^2$, where $e_n = |x_n - \bar x|$ is the error with respect to the zero $\bar x$. If $e_n \approx 10^{-3}$, what is $e_{n+1}$ approximately?
  1. $10^{-4}$
  2. $\frac12 \cdot 10^{-3}$
  3. $10^{-9}$
  4. $10^{-6}$
Explanation. $(10^{-3})^2 = 10^{-6}$: at each step the number of correct digits roughly doubles. $\frac12 \cdot 10^{-3}$ would be the (linear) behaviour of bisection.
am1-f-632 · level facile · notes
Newton's method $x_{n+1} = x_n - \frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}$ for $f(x) = e^x - 2$, with $x_0 = 0$: what is $x_1$?
  1. $1$
  2. $2$
  3. $-1$
  4. $\log 2$
Explanation. $f(0) = -1$ and $f'(0) = e^0 = 1$, so $x_1 = 0 - \frac{-1}{1} = 1$. The exact zero is $\log 2 \approx 0.69$.
am1-f-633 · level facile · notes
Geometric meaning of Newton's method for $f(x) = 0$: the point $x_{n+1}$ is the abscissa where the $x$-axis is met by:
  1. the secant line through $(x_{n-1}, f(x_{n-1}))$ and $(x_n, f(x_n))$
  2. the tangent line to the graph of $f$ at $(x_n, f(x_n))$
  3. the horizontal line through $(x_n, f(x_n))$
  4. the normal line to the graph of $f$ at $(x_n, f(x_n))$
Explanation. The tangent $y = f(x_n) + f'(x_n)(x - x_n)$ vanishes at $x = x_n - \frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}$. The secant gives the secant method.
am1-f-634 · level facile · notes
Applied to $f(x) = \frac1x - a$ (with $a > 0$, zero at $\frac1a$), Newton's method $x_{n+1} = x_n - \frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}$ gives the iteration:
  1. $x_{n+1} = x_n - a\,x_n^2$
  2. $x_{n+1} = \frac{x_n}{2 - a\,x_n}$
  3. $x_{n+1} = x_n\,(2 - a\,x_n)$
  4. $x_{n+1} = x_n\,(1 + a\,x_n)$
Explanation. $f'(x) = -\frac{1}{x^2}$, so $x_{n+1} = x_n + x_n^2\left(\frac{1}{x_n} - a\right) = 2x_n - a\,x_n^2$: it computes $\frac1a$ without divisions.

Part: Series (52)

1. Numerical series (12)

am1-f-125 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about numerical series is correct?
  1. If $a_k \rr 0$, then $\sum a_k$ is convergent.
  2. If $\sum a_k$ is convergent, then $\{a_k\}$ is decreasing.
  3. If $\sum a_k$ is divergent, then $a_k \not\rr 0$.
  4. If $\sum a_k$ is convergent, then $\lim_{k \rr \ip} a_k = 0$.
Explanation. This is the necessary condition for convergence: $a_k = s_k - s_{k-1} \rr s - s = 0$. It is not sufficient: the harmonic series diverges although $\frac1k \rr 0$.
am1-f-126 · level facile · notes
What is the sum of the telescoping series $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{1}{k\,(k+1)}$?
  1. $1$
  2. $\frac12$
  3. $\ip$
  4. $2$
Explanation. $\frac{1}{k(k+1)} = \frac1k - \frac1{k+1} = b_k - b_{k+1}$ with $b_k = \frac1k \rr 0$: the sum is $b_1 - 0 = 1$.
am1-f-127 · level facile · notes
The geometric series $\sum_{k=0}^{\infty} (-1)^k$ is:
  1. convergent with sum $\frac12$
  2. divergent to $\ip$
  3. irregular
  4. convergent with sum $0$
Explanation. With ratio $q = -1 \le -1$ the geometric series is irregular: the partial sums alternate between $1$ and $0$. The formula $\frac{1}{1-q}$ holds only for $|q| < 1$.
am1-f-397 · level facile · notes
What is the sum of the geometric series $\sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \left(\frac23\right)^k$?
  1. $\frac23$
  2. $2$
  3. $3$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. For $|q| < 1$: $\sum_{k=0}^\infty q^k = \frac{1}{1 - q} = \frac{1}{1/3} = 3$.
am1-f-398 · level facile · notes
The harmonic series $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac1k$:
  1. diverges to $\ip$
  2. converges to $1$
  3. converges to $2$
  4. is irregular
